{\sf WeightedEdges}
.
∎

Figure 10:
Examples of a blue edge (above) and red edge (below) w.r.t. a drawing from
Definition

5
.

This figure also illustrates the notion of left and right from
Definition

7
. The crossing between the two green edges is to the left of the blue edge, and that between two pink edges is to its right. The crossing between the two brown edges is neither to its left nor to its right, because (at least) one of the two brown edges crosses the blue edge.

4.1
Algorithm

We use
n
and
m
to denote the number of vertices and edges (with multiplicities), respectively.
6
6

6

Note that if we start with the kernelized instance, then
n
and
m
are polynomial in the original parameter
k^{\star}
; however we will only use the kernelization to derive our final theorem.
Next, we perform
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
guesses in the original instance in order to transform it into an extended instance. This increases the number of vertices and edges by at most
2
, and from
2
it follows that the original instance is a
Yes
-instance iff for at least one of the guesses, we have an
Yes
-instance. For each extended instance corresponding to each of the guesses, we will run the following algorithm.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(G,V_{1},V_{2},{\sf ExtBnd},\pi,k)
be the extended instance given as an input. Let
c
be a sufficiently large constant.
When
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, our algorithm directly solves the given instance by a careful enumeration.
Otherwise, when

k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, we are in the recursive case, explained later.

Base case.

At a high level, we want to guess the subset of at most
2k
crossing edges and the ordering of their endpoints, and check whether the rest of the graph (specifically, each connected component in the graph) can be drawn without crossings, such that it respects the given partial orders
\pi
and other constraints. However, due to the way we count the crossings between the multi-edges incident to the different endpoints of a boundary edge (
Footnote

5
in
Definition

3
), it may happen that a drawing contains crossings; but we do not want to count such drawings. To handle this subtlety, we proceed as follows.

Suppose
\mathcal{I}
is a
Yes
-instance, then let
\sigma
be a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Let
X\subseteq E(G)
be the set of at most
2k
edges involved in crossings in
\sigma
– note that the crossings are counted according to
Definition

3
. First, we guess
X
, for which there are at most
\binom{m}{k}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
choices, since
k=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})
. Let
Y^{a}\coloneqq{\sf Endpoints}(X)
, and note that
|Y^{a}|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})
. Next, let
Y^{b}\coloneqq{\sf Endpoints}({\sf WeightedEdges})
. Note that due to
Item

6
in
Definition

2
, it follows that
|Y^{b}|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})
. Let
Y\coloneqq Y^{a}\cup Y^{b}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
. Let
Y_{i}=Y\cap V_{i}
for
i\in[2]
. We guess a permutation
\zeta_{i}
of
Y_{i}
that is compatible with the given ordering
\pi
, and let
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
. Note that the number of choices for
\zeta
are bounded by
(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}}))!=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}}\log(k^{\star}))}
. Henceforth, we focus on the guess where the guessed permutation
\zeta
is compatible with
\sigma
.

Then, we use the algorithm of
Proposition

2

(
[
3
]
) to find, for each connected component
C
in
G-(X\cup{\sf WeightedEdges})
a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}(C)
without any crossings that respects the given partial order
\pi
restricted to
C
, if such a drawing exists. This can be done in polynomial time due to
Proposition

2
. If such a drawing does not exist for some component
C
, then we terminate this guess. Otherwise, we obtain a combined drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
of
G
by gluing together the orderings of components
C
and
X
in an appropriate order, that respects the given partial ordering
\pi
.

On the other hand, if for all guesses
X
and the ordering of the endpoints of edges in
X
, if either the number of crossings is larger than
k
, or if at least one connected component of
G-X
cannot be drawn without crossings, then we return that
\mathcal{I}
is a no-instance. From the above discussion, the following lemma easily follows.

This completes the description of how we handle the first base case when
k<c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an extended instance of
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
with underlying graph
G
, and parameter
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, where
c
is a sufficiently large constant. Then, the algorithm returns a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
iff
\mathcal{I}
is an extended
Yes
-instance.

Otherwise, we proceed to the recursive case, explained next.

Recursive case.

Since the base case is not applicable, we know that
k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
.
At a high level, our recursive algorithm consists of the following steps.

1.

Divide.
First, we prove some combinatorial properties of a “balanced separator” in this step, which is defined by a set of two edges, called
lsep
,
rsep
. This is a balanced separator, in that the number of crossings to the left of
lsep
and that to the right of
rsep
, is at most
k/2
; in addition it has certain additional nice properties. This lets us divide the instance into three parts – left, middle, and right. Of these, the left and the right parts become sub-instances (in fact, extended instances) with parameters bounded by
k/2
, whereas the middle part has certain special properties, which lets us solve it efficiently.

2.

Guess the interfaces.
In this step, we “guess” the two separator edges, as well as other auxiliary sets of vertices and edges, whose existence is showed in the previous step. For this, we have
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices.

3.

Creating sub-instances.
For each of the guesses made in the previous step, we formally create the two sub-instances, which takes polynomial time.

4.

Conquer left and right.
For each such pair of sub-instances corresponding to
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k}))}
guesses, we recursively call the algorithm on the left and right sub-instances. Since the parameter is bounded by
k/2
in each sub-instance, this inductively takes

n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot T(k/2)\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k}))}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k/2})}

time.

5.

Obtaining a combined drawing.
Assuming that for some guess, both the recursive calls corresponding to the left and right instances output the corresponding drawings, then we combine the drawings—along with the guesses made for the middle subproblem—in order to obtain a combined drawing for the current instance. This takes polynomial time.

Let
T_{2}(n,k)
denote an upper bound on the running time of our algorithm on an extended instance on a graph with at most
n
vertices and parameter bounded by
k
. From the above description, it follows that
T_{2}(n,k)
satisfies the following recurrence.
7
7

7

We will give a formal proof of this after giving a formal description of the algorithm.

\displaystyle T_{2}(n,k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}&\text{ if }k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\\
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot T_{2}(n,\frac{k}{2})+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}&\text{ if }k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\end{cases}

(1)

It can be shown that this recurrence solves to
T_{2}(n,k^{\star})=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
.

4.2
Step 1: Divide

Definition 6

(Ordering between edges)
.

See
Figure

10
.
Let
e_{a},e_{b}
be two distinct edges in
E
. Further, let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, where each
\pi_{i}
is a (partial) order on
V_{i}
, for
i\in[2]
. We say that
e_{a}
is
to the left of
e_{b}
w.r.t.
\pi
if for each
i\in[2]
, we have that
{\sf endpt}(e_{a},i)\leq{\sf endpt}(e_{b},i)
, with at least one inequality being strict (since
e_{a}\neq e_{b}
). In this case, we also say that
e_{b}
is
to the right of

e_{a}
.

Note that “left of” and “right of” relations are partial orders on the edges.

Observation 4
.

Let
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, where each
\tau_{i}
is permutation of
V_{i}
for
i\in[2]
. Then, for any pair of distinct edges
e_{a},e_{b}\in E(G)
, either
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
cross, or one of the two edges is to the left of the other w.r.t.
\tau
. In particular, for any blue edge
b\in E(G)
, except a subset of at most
\sqrt{k}
edges of
E
that cross
b
in
\pi
, each edge in
E_{12}
is either to the left of
b
or to the right of
b
w.r.t.
\pi
.

Definition 7

(Ordering between an edge and a crossing)
.

Let
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
be a drawing of an instance
\mathcal{I}
with an underlying graph
G
. Consider an edge
e\in E(G)
. We say that a crossing between edges
e_{a},e_{b}\in E(G)
(possibly
e=e_{a}
or
e=e_{b}
but not both)
is to the left (resp. right) of
e
w.r.t.
\sigma
if (i)
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
cross in
\sigma
, and (ii) both
e_{a}
and
e_{b}
are to the left (resp. right) of
e
w.r.t.
\sigma
. See
Figure

10
.

Note that when
\sigma
is clear from the context, we will omit “according to
\sigma
” when using left/right relations. For the rest of this section, we will fix an instance
\mathcal{I}
of
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
that is an
Yes
-instance for
k
crossings, but is a
No
-instance for at most
k-1
crossings. We refer to this type of instance as a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance
. We now introduce the following crucial definition of a
separator
.

Definition 8
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, and let
\sigma
be any drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. We say that
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}
is a
separator
w.r.t.
\sigma
, if the following properties hold (see
Figure

11
).

1.

{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\in E(G)
are distinct blue edges w.r.t.
\sigma
and they do not cross each other.

2.

The number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}
, and that to the right of
{\sf rsep}
are each at most
\frac{k}{2}
.

3.

\blacklozenge
Define

{\sf Mid}_{i}\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{i}:\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},i))\leq\sigma_{i}(u)\leq\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},i))\right\}.

That is,
{\sf Mid}_{i}
is the set of vertices that are drawn between the respective endpoints of the two edges
{\sf lsep}
and
{\sf rsep}
.

For
i\in[2]
, it holds that
|{\sf Mid}_{i}|<4\sqrt{k}+2
.

4.

|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
, where

{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)\coloneqq\left\{uv\in E:u\in{\sf Mid}_{1},v\in{\sf Mid}_{2}\right\}\setminus\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}.

5.

|{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
, where
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)\coloneqq{\sf cross}({\sf lsep},\sigma)\cup{\sf cross}({\sf rsep},\sigma)

Figure 11:
Illustration of a separator from
Definition

8
.
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}
shown in
blue
. Edges of
M\coloneqq{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
are shown in
brown
, and those of
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})
shown in
olive
(these are the edges that cross
{\sf lsep}
or
{\sf rsep}
). Vertices of
{\sf Mid}_{1}
are shown in
dark green
and those of
{\sf Mid}_{2}
in
light pink
. Vertices of
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
are shown in
purple
.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, and let
\sigma
be any drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Then, there exists a separator
{\sf Sep}
w.r.t.
\sigma
.

Proof.

Let us define a lexicographic order
\prec
on the edges of
E
using the order of their endpoints in
V_{1}
using
\sigma_{1}
, with ties broken using the endpoints in
V_{2}
using
\sigma_{2}
. More formally, for two distinct edges
e_{a},e_{b}\in E
, we have
e_{a}\prec e_{b}
iff
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},1))<\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},1))
, or
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},1))=\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},1))
and
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},2))<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},2))
(multi-edges are treated as a single edge for this order). Let
{\sf rsep}\in E(G)
be the first blue edge, according to
\prec
, that has more than
\frac{k}{2}
crossings to its left. First we claim that
{\sf rsep}
exists, since
{\sf rightbound}
—which is blue, and has all (
k>\frac{k}{2}
) crossings to its left— satisfies the stated property.

By the definition of
{\sf rsep}
, the number of crossings to its left is larger than
\frac{k}{2}
, which implies the following: (1) the number of crossings to its right is at most
\frac{k}{2}
(note that some crossings may not be either to the left or to the right of
{\sf rsep}
), and (2) the number of edges to its left is larger than
\frac{k}{2}
. Further, (2) implies that
{\sf leftbound}
is one of the edges that is to the left of
{\sf rsep}
, since
{\sf leftbound}
is to the left of every other edge. In particular, this implies that
{\sf rsep}\neq{\sf leftbound}
. See
Figure

11
for an illustration.

Now, we define
{\sf lsep}
to be the last edge according to
\prec
such that: (i)
{\sf lsep}
is blue, (ii)
{\sf lsep}\prec{\sf rsep}
, and (iii)
{\sf lsep}
does not cross
{\sf rsep}
according to
\sigma
. First,
{\sf lsep}
exists because
{\sf leftbound}
satisfies conditions (i) - (iii) – indeed
{\sf leftbound}
is not equal to
{\sf rsep}
as shown above. Further, since
{\sf lsep}\prec{\sf rsep}
,
{\sf lsep}
is to the left of
{\sf rsep}
. Now, if the number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}
is larger than
\frac{k}{2}
, then this contradicts the choice of
{\sf rsep}
as the lexicographically first blue edge with more than
\frac{k}{2}
crossings to its left. Therefore, the number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}
is at most
\frac{k}{2}
. This shows that
{\sf Sep}\coloneqq\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}
satisfies the first two properties from
Definition

8
. It remains to show that it also satisfies the third property from
Definition

8
. Before this, we will show that
|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
, as claimed in the statement of the lemma.

Let us use
M\coloneqq{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
for brevity, where the latter is defined in
Definition

8
. Suppose for the contradiction
|M|>2\sqrt{k}
. Note that the definition of
M
, for each edge
e\in M
,
{\sf lsep}\prec e\prec{\sf rsep}
, and
e
does not cross
{\sf rsep}
. Then, by
3
,
M
contains at least one blue edge, say
b
. However, note that
b
satisfies conditions (i)–(iii) above, which contradicts the choice of
{\sf lsep}
, since
{\sf lsep}
is the lexicographically last such edge. This shows that
|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
.

\blacklozenge
Now we will show the bound on the number of vertices in
{\sf Mid}_{1},{\sf Mid}_{2}
. Note that this part is relevant only for normal instances. Note that
|{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)|\leq 2
and
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)\subseteq{\sf Mid}_{1}
, which accounts for the
+2
factor in the bound. So now, consider any vertex
x\in{\sf Mid}_{1}\setminus{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)
. Since
G
is a connected graph, there is at least one edge incident to
x
, say it is
xy
with
y\in V_{2}
. Consider one such edge. If
y\in{\sf Mid}_{2}
, then
xy\in M
, and
|M|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
. Therefore, the number of vertices in
{\sf Mid}_{1}
with at least one endpoint in
{\sf Mid}_{2}
is bounded by
2\sqrt{k}
. Otherwise,
y
satisfies either
\sigma_{2}(y)<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
or
\sigma_{2}(y)>\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)))
. Therefore, it follows that the edge
xy
crosses either
{\sf lsep}
or
{\sf rsep}
. However, since
{\sf lsep}
and
{\sf rsep}
are blue edges, the number of edges crossing either of them is bounded by
\sqrt{k}
, which implies that the number of vertices in
{\sf Mid}_{1}
without a neighbor in
{\sf Mid}_{2}
is bounded by
2\sqrt{k}
. Therefore,
|{\sf Mid}_{1}|\leq 2+2\sqrt{k}+2\sqrt{k}=2+4\sqrt{k}
. An analogous argument shows the same bound on
|{\sf Mid}_{2}|
.

∎

We introduce the following definition that will be later useful for proving the correctness of the algorithm.

Definition 9
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
Yes
-instance with underlying graph
G
, and let
\sigma
be a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Consider a separator
{\sf Sep}
w.r.t.
\sigma
along with the associated objects as in
Definition

8
(see
Figure

11
). Then, we define the following division of vertices on each layer into left (L), middle (M), and right (R) parts, as follows.

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}(u)\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)\leq\sigma_{1}(u)\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1))\leq\sigma_{1}(u)\right\}

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\right\}

Finally, for each
{\cal T}\in\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
, define
V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})\coloneqq V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})\cup V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})
.

Specifically, in
Figure

11
, these sets correspond to the vertices belonging to the yellow, cyan, and light green shaded regions (along with the shared vertices of
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
), respectively.

Henceforth, until the beginning of
Section

4.2.1
, we fix a
hypothetical
drawing
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
and the corresponding separator
{\sf Sep}
corresponding to a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance
\mathcal{I}
, as guaranteed by
Lemma

2
, and the associated objects with
{\sf Sep}
from
Definition

8
. These qualifiers/properties of
\sigma
and
{\sf Sep}
will not be repeated in the definitions and lemma statements that follow, until the beginning of
Section

4.2.1
.

In the following definition, we classify the components attached to a vertex in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
into multiple classes. Components of each class satisfy certain properties, which will be subsequently used to derive more information on the number of such components, or the way in which they must be drawn.

Definition 10
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(G,V_{1},V_{2},{\sf ExtBnd},\pi,k)
be an extended instance of
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
and
\sigma
be a hypothetical drawing. Furthermore, let
{\sf Sep}
be the separator corresponding to
\mathcal{I}
.
Let
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
be a vertex, and let
{\sf comps}_{G}(v)
denote the set of connected components of
C_{v}-v
, where
C_{v}
is the connected component of
G
that contains
v
. We partition these components into multiple classes (subsets), called
{\sf Special}(v),\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v),{\sf Pendants}(v)
, and
{\sf UpDown}(v)
(we may omit the subscript
G
, if it is clear from the context). A component
C\in{\sf comps}_{G}(v)
belongs to:

•

{\sf Special}(v)
, if it contains at least one edge of
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
, or a vertex of
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
.

•

\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, if
C\not\in{\sf Special}(v)
, and
C
contains a vertex
u\in\bigcup_{i\in[2]}\bigcup_{j\in[\lambda]}V^{j}_{i}

8
8

8

Recall that
V^{j}_{i}
’s are the
\lambda=\mathcal{O}(\log k^{\star})
subsets over which the total orders
\pi^{j}_{i}
are defined, and these
\lambda
total orders constitute
\pi_{i}
. Furthermore,
\bigcup_{i\in[2]}\bigcup_{j\in[\lambda]}V^{j}_{i}
may not be equal to
V(G)

•

{\sf Pendants}(v)
, if it does not belong to
{\sf Special}(v)
or
\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, and
C
consists of only a single
pendant vertex

u
attached to
v
. Note that the edge
uv
could belong to
{\sf WeightedEdges}
.

•

{\sf UpDown}(v)
, if it does not belong to
{\sf Special}(v)
or
\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, and contains vertices of both
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.

See
Figure

12
for an illustration.

Figure 12:
Illustration of classification of components from
Definition

10
.
v,w
are two vertices from
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
.
C_{1}

\in{\sf comps}(v)
belongs to
{\sf Special}(v)
since it contains an edge crossing
{\sf lsep}
. Three components
C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}

\in{\sf comps}(w)
consist of pendant vertices attached to
w
. Finally,
C_{5}

\in{\sf comps}(w)
belongs to
{\sf UpDown}(w)
since it contains a vertex in the same layer as
w
.

First, we have the following observation.

Observation 5
.

For any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, it holds that
|{\sf Special}(v)|\leq|{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})|+|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})|+2\leq 4\sqrt{k}+2
.

Next, we bound the number of components in
{\sf UpDown}(v)
in the following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

For any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, it holds that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 4\sqrt{k}
.

Proof.

Fix any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
. Suppose w.l.o.g. that
v\in V_{1}
since
V_{2}
case is symmetric. Suppose for the contradiction that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|>4\sqrt{k}
.

Consider some component
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
. Note that
C
must contain a vertex, say
{\sf upper}(C)\in V_{2}
, that is adjacent to
v
, as well as a vertex
{\sf lower}(C)
, such that
{\sf lower}(C)\in V_{1}
and is adjacent to
{\sf upper}(C)
(since
C
does not belong to
{\sf Pendants}(v)
). Define
{\sf upper}(C)
and
{\sf lower}(C)
for each
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
in this manner (in case of multiple choices for such vertices, select arbitrarily).

For any
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
, since
{\sf lower}(C)\in V_{1}
and
v\in V_{1}
, either
{\sf lower}(C)
is placed to the left of
v
or to the right of
v
according to
\sigma_{1}
. By pigeonhole principle, either the number of
{\sf lower}(C)
’s to the left or right of
u
is larger than
2\sqrt{k}
. W.l.o.g., suppose the number of components such that
{\sf lower}(C)
is to the right of
v
is
r>2\sqrt{k}
. Let us order the vertices
{\sf lower}(C)
corresponding to such components, as
{\sf lower}_{1},{\sf lower}_{2},\ldots,{\sf lower}_{r}
according to their left-to-right order in
\sigma_{1}
, and let
{\sf upper}_{1},{\sf upper}_{2},\ldots,{\sf upper}_{r}
be the corresponding
{\sf upper}(C)
vertices (note that this need not be their left-to-right order according to
\sigma_{2}
). Note that
{\sf lower}_{j}
’s and
{\sf upper}_{j}
’s are distinct, since they belong to different connected components in
G-v
. We will show that the number of crossings between the edges
E_{r}\coloneqq\left\{(v,{\sf upper}_{i}),({\sf lower}_{i},{\sf upper}_{i}):1\leq i\leq r\right\}
is at least
\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\geq\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}>k
, which will yield the desired contradiction.

We prove the claim using induction, where we show that for any
i\geq 1
, the number of crossings among the edges of
E_{i}\coloneqq\left\{(v,{\sf upper}_{j}),({\sf lower}_{j},{\sf upper}_{j}):1\leq j\leq i\right\}
is at least
\frac{i(i-1)}{2}
. The base case for
i=1
is trivial, since there are exactly
0
crossings among the edges of
E_{1}
– since both the edges in
E_{1}
are incident to
{\sf upper}_{1}
. Now, suppose the claim is true for some
i\geq 1
. Let
U_{i}\coloneqq\left\{{\sf upper}_{j}:1\leq j\leq i\right\}
, and let
U_{i}^{l},U^{r}_{i}
denote the set of vertices from
U
that are to the left and right of
{\sf upper}_{i+1}
, respectively. Notice that, for each
{\sf upper}_{j}\in U^{l}_{i}
,
\sigma_{2}({\sf upper}_{j})<\sigma_{2}({\sf upper}_{i+1})
and
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}({\sf upper}_{j})
, hence the edge
(v,{\sf upper}_{i+1})
crosses the edge
({\sf lower}_{j},{\sf upper}_{j})
for each
{\sf upper}_{j}\in U^{l}_{i}
. On the other hand, for each
{\sf upper}_{j}\in U^{r}_{i}
,
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}({\sf lower}_{j})<\sigma_{1}({\sf lower}_{i+1})
and
\sigma_{2}({\sf upper}_{i+1})<\sigma_{2}({\sf upper}_{j})
, hence the edge
({\sf lower}_{i+1},{\sf upper}_{i+1})
crosses both
(v,{\sf upper}_{j})
as well
({\sf lower}_{j},{\sf upper}_{j})
, for each
{\sf upper}_{j}\in U^{r}_{i}
. Therefore, the number of crossings, where one edge is from
E_{i}
and the other edge is from
\left\{(v,{\sf upper}_{i+1}),({\sf upper}_{i+1},{\sf lower}_{i+1})\right\}
is at least
l+2r\geq l+r=i
(see
Figure

13
). Then, by using the inductive hypothesis, the number of crossings among the edges of
E_{i+1}
is at least
\frac{i(i-1)}{2}+i=\frac{i(i+1)}{2}
. This finishes the proof.

Figure 13:
Illustration for the inductive proof in
Lemma

3
. Here, the vertices of
U^{l}_{i}
—which are to the left of
{\sf upper}^{i+1}
—are shown in
teal
and that of
U_{i}^{r}
—which are to the right of
{\sf upper}^{i+1}
—are shown in
orange
color.

∎

At this point, we are left with analyzing the behavior of components in
{\sf Pendants}(v)
. This depends on the structure of the separator
{\sf Sep}(\sigma)
guaranteed by
Lemma

2
. In the following definition, we study three different cases.

Figure 14:
Illustration of three different cases from
Definition

11

Definition 11
.

Let
{\sf Sep}(\sigma)=\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}
be the separator as guaranteed by
Lemma

2
. We have the following
two different (non-exclusive) cases.

(i)
Distinct Top (DT)
.

When
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
.

(ii)
Distinct Bottom (DB)
.

When
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
.

Based on this, we now have three different mutually exclusive cases (see
Figure

14
).

1.
Distinct Endpoints (DE) case
.

When both
DT
and
DB
hold.

2.
Shared Bottom (SB) case
.

When
DT
holds but
DB
does not. That is,
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
and
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
.

3.
Shared Top (ST) case
.

When
DB
holds but
DT
does not. That is,
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
and
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
.

Lemma 4
.

The following properties hold in the drawing
\sigma
.

1.

In
DT case
,

(a)

All the vertices of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
are placed to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)
.

(b)

All the vertices of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2))
are placed to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
.

2.

In
DB case
,

(c)

All the vertices of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))
are placed to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
.

(d)

All the vertices of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1))
are placed to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
.

Thus, in
DE case
, all (a), (b), (c), (d) hold. In
SB case
, (a), and (b) hold. In
ST case
, (c) and (d) hold.

Proof.

First let us consider
DT case
. Suppose that there exists a pendant vertex
w\in{\sf Pendants}(u_{2})
attached to
u_{2}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
that lies to the right of
u_{1}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)
. That is,
\sigma_{1}(u_{1})<\sigma_{1}(w)
. Let
u^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
, and
u^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
.
Now, either (i)
\sigma_{1}(w)<\sigma_{1}(u^{\prime}_{1})
, or (ii)
\sigma_{1}(u^{\prime}_{1})<\sigma_{1}(w)
. In case (i),
w\in{\sf Mid}_{1}
and thus
u_{2}w\in{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
, contradicting that
w\not\in{\sf Special}(u_{2})
. In case (ii), the edge
u_{2}w
crosses
{\sf rsep}=(u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2})
, since
\sigma_{1}(u_{1})<\sigma_{1}(u^{\prime}_{1})
and
\sigma_{1}(u^{\prime}_{1})<\sigma_{1}(w)
. This implies that
u_{2}w\in{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})
, again contradicting that
w\not\in{\sf Special}(u_{2})
. Similarly, one can prove (b) in
DT case
, and (c) and (d) in
DB
case.

Now in
DE case
, both
DT
and
DB
hold, which implies properties (a)–(d). In
SB
case, only
DT
holds, which implies properties (a) and (b). Finally, in
ST case
, only
DB
holds, which implies properties (c) and (d).
∎

Thus, we are left with analyzing the behavior of some of the pendant components in
SB
and
ST
cases. Specifically, in
SB
case, when
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
, we need to analyze which components in
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))
(which is the same as
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1))
) are placed to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
, and which of them are placed to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
. Note that there cannot be any that are placed in between the two vertices, since otherwise they would belong to
{\sf Special}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))
. Analogously, in
ST
case, when
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
, we need to analyze which components in
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
(which is the same as
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
) are placed to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)
, and which of them are placed to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
. In the next subsection, we will focus on
SB
case, and the
ST
case will be handled analogously. These cases are not equivalent due to weight, and, also, we need to take care of the placement of these pendant components to preserve the number of crossings on both sides. In short, we are going to show that all that matters is the number of these components on both sides and not the components itself.

4.2.1
Handling
SB case

In this subsection, we will make some simplifying assumptions. First, we are in
SB case
, when
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
, and for simplicity, we denote this shared endpoint by
v
. Similarly, let
u^{l}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
and
u^{r}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
. If a component
C\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
, then it contains a pendant vertex
w
attached to
v
. In this case, we will use the vertex
w
and the component
C
interchangeably. Note here, that the edge
vw
in a pendant component may be a multi-edge with weight/multiplicity
\ell\geq 0
, in which case we also say that
\ell
is the multiplicity of
C
or
w
. If
vw
is not a multi-edge, then we define the multiplicity to be
1
.

In the following definition, we introduce the notion of nice families of drawings.

Definition 12
.

Let the shared endpoint be
v
and let
u^{l}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
and
u^{r}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
.

•

A
pendant-blueprint
is a tuple
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=({\sf LeftPNum},{\sf RightPNum})
, where
{\sf LeftPNum}+{\sf RightPNum}={\sf TotalPNum}
, where
{\sf TotalPNum}
denotes the total sum of multiplicities of all pendants in
{\sf Pendants}(v)
.

•

A
pendant-guess
is a partition
{\sf PendantGuess}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
. That is, the partition of components of

{\sf Pendants}(v)
into left and right side, respectively. See
Figure

15
for an illustration.

•

Let
{\sf PendantGuess}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
be a pendant guess, and let
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
be a pendant blueprint. We say that
{\sf PendantGuess}
is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant
if it satisfies that, the total sum of multiplicities of vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}
is equal to
{\sf LeftPNum}
, and the total sum of multiplicities of vertices in
{\sf RightPendants}
is equal to
{\sf RightPNum}
.

•

Let
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=({\sf LeftPNum},{\sf RightPNum})
be a pendant-blueprint, and let
{\sf PendantGuess}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
be a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant pendant-guess. Then, we define a
{\sf NiceFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess},v,\sigma)
to be a family of drawings
\sigma^{\prime}=(\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime})
satisfying the following properties.

1.

\sigma_{1}(w)=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}(w)
for all
w\in V_{1}
, i.e., the order of the vertices on the first layer in the drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
is exactly the same as that of
\sigma
,

2.

\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}
, when restricted to the vertices of
V_{2}\setminus{\sf Pendants}(v)
are the same,

3.

All the pendants in
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
are placed to the left of
u^{l}
in
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
.

4.

All the pendants in
{\sf RightPendants}(v)
are placed to the right of
u^{r}
in
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
.

Figure 15:
A separator in
SB case
, where
v={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
. A partition
({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
, which can be thought of as a
PendantGuess
. It is compliant with
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=(4,3)
, assuming that the multiplicity of each edge is
1
. (Note that the other two endpoints of
{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}
are not in
{\sf Pendants}(v)
, since the corresponding components belong to
{\sf Special}(v)
according to
Definition

10
.)

We also have the following definition.

Definition 13
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be drawings with
k
crossings. Let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
. We say that
\sigma^{\prime}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible, if it satisfies the following properties.

•

{\sf Sep}
is also a separator w.r.t.
\sigma^{\prime}
.

•

{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
.

•

{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
.

Next, we state the following lemma.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, let
\sigma
be a drawing with
k
crossings, and let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
with
SB case
. Let
v={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
.

Then, there exists a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
such that, for any
{\sf PendantGuess}
that is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant, there exists a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf NiceFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess},v,\sigma)
such that
\sigma^{\prime}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible.

Intuitive explanation of the statement of the lemma.

Before proving the lemma, let us illustrate what the lemma is trying to say with the help of an example of
Figure

15
. Suppose the drawing
\sigma
distributes
{\sf Pendants}(v)
into left and right as illustrated in the example. As mentioned in the caption,
{\sf PendantGuess}=(\left\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\right\},\left\{v_{5},v_{6},v_{7}\right\})
is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant, where
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=(4,3)
.
Now consider another
{\sf PendantGuess}^{\prime}=(\left\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{6},v_{7}\right\},

\left\{v_{1},v_{4},v_{5}\right\})
, which is also
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant. Then, there exists a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
such that, (i)
\sigma^{\prime}
places
\left\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{6},v_{7}\right\}
on the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
,
\left\{v_{1},v_{4},v_{5}\right\}
to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
, and does not change the ordering of the rest of the vertices (this is captured by the condition that
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf NiceFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess}^{\prime},v,\sigma)
); and (ii)
{\sf Sep}
and its associated objects remain unchanged in
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
(this is captured by
\sigma^{\prime}
being
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible). Now let us prove the lemma formally.

Proof of
Lemma

5
.

Let
w^{\star}\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
, such that the edge
vw^{\star}
is crossed
q
times in
\sigma
. We claim that each edge
vw
for any
w\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
is
also
crossed exactly
q
times in
\sigma
. Suppose for the contradiction that there exists some
w^{\prime}\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
such that the edge
vw^{\prime}
is crossed
q^{\prime}<q
times in
\sigma
(if
q^{\prime}>q
, then swap the roles of
w
and
w^{\prime}
in the following). Then, the drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
is obtained by placing
w
just after
w^{\prime}
. If
\mu(vw)>0
is the multiplicity of the edge
vw
, then the number of crossings in
\sigma^{\prime}
is now
k-\mu(vw)\cdot q+\mu(vw)\cdot q^{\prime}<k
. Thus,
\sigma^{\prime}
is a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with fewer than
k
crossings, contradicting the
k
-minimality of
\mathcal{I}
.

Let
{\sf PendantBlueprint}\coloneqq({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum})
. Here,
{\sf LeftNum}
(
{\sf RightNum})
denotes the total sum of multiplicities of all pendants in
{\sf Pendants}(v)
that are placed to the left of (resp. right of)
u^{l}={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
(resp.
u^{r}={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
) in
\sigma_{2}
.

Consider any pendant
w^{l}\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
that is placed to the left of
u^{l}
according
\sigma_{2}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{2}(w^{l})<\sigma_{2}(u^{l})
. Similarly, let
w^{r}\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
that is placed to the right of
u^{r}
according to
\sigma_{2}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{2}(w^{r})>\sigma_{2}(u^{r})
. We will use
w^{l}
and
w^{r}
in defining
\sigma^{\prime}
in the next paragraph.

Now consider any
{\sf PendantGuess}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
that is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant. We claim that, the drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
obtained by modifying
\sigma
in the following way, satisfies the claimed properties: (1) relocate all the vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}
in the same place as
w^{l}
9
9

9

More formally, if
a^{l},b^{l}\in V_{2}\setminus{\sf Pendants}(v)
are the vertices that are immediately to the left and right of
w^{l}
in
\sigma_{2}
, respectively, then we place all the vertices of
{\sf LeftPendants}
between
a^{l}
and
b^{l}
in an arbitrary order.
, (2) place all the vertices in
{\sf RightPendants}
in the same place as
w^{r}
, and (3) the order of all other vertices remains unchanged. First, we observe that
\sigma^{\prime}
thus obtained indeed belongs to
{\sf NiceFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess},v,\sigma)
. Furthermore, since each edge
vw
for
w\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
is crossed exactly the same number of times in
\sigma
, it follows that the relocation of vertices used to obtain
\sigma^{\prime}
does not change the number of times each edge is crossed in
\sigma
or
\sigma^{\prime}
. Furthermore, note that
\sigma
already places
{\sf LeftNum}
(resp.
{\sf RightNum}
) pendants (counting multiplicities) from
{\sf Pendants}(v)
to the left (resp. right) of
u^{l}
(resp.
u^{r}
), and the resulting drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
does not alter this either. Therefore,
{\sf Sep}
also satisfies all properties from
Definition

8
w.r.t.
\sigma^{\prime}
as well. This shows that
\sigma^{\prime}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible.
∎

4.3
Step 2. Guessing the Interfaces

In this step, we will “guess” various subsets of edges and vertices that can be used in the creation of two sub-instances in the next step. Each of the guesses is made by assuming that
\mathcal{I}
is a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance with hypothetical solution drawing
\sigma
. These guesses will be made in multiple steps, with each step dependent on all possible choices of the previous steps. These guesses can be thought of as
branching
for each of the choices specified in all steps. Furthermore, if any of the guesses in a subsequent step contradicts the constraints imposed on a drawing (as in
Definition

3
), or a guess made in a prior step, then such a guess is considered invalid and terminated (i.e., that branch is not explored further). If all the guesses are terminated, or the corresponding recursive calls report that the sub-instances are
No
-instances, then we will conclude that our assumption that
\mathcal{I}
was a
Yes
-instance was wrong, and thus we must have a
No
-instance.

In each step, we specify at a high level the kind of object that we are trying to guess. In this algorithm, we label vertices using one or more labels from the following set
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
, that are indicative of the sub-instance(s) that the vertex will belong to.
10
10

10

We do not explicitly define a middle sub-instance in the algorithm, but the label
{\cal M}
is defined for convenience.
Whenever we are trying to guess a subset of edges, we follow the following convention: all the edges of a multi-edge are treated in the same manner, such as being chosen in a guess.

Step A: Separator.

We start by guessing
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}\right\}\subseteq E
that is a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
. Note that there are at most
m^{2}=n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
choices. Furthermore, based on our guess, we can also conclude whether we are in
DE, SB,
or
ST case
, which will be important later. Next, we guess
{\sf Mid}_{i}\subseteq V_{i}\setminus{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},i)
for
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
, such that each is of size
4\sqrt{k}+2
. There are at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices for this. Overall, we have at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices.

Step B: Labeling
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
.

DE case.

Vertices in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf lsep})
receive label
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M}\right\}
, and that in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf rsep})
receive
\left\{{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
.

SB case.

{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
,
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M}\right\}
, and
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
.

ST case.

{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
,
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M}\right\}
, and
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},1)
receives labels
\left\{{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
.

Step C: Labeling
{\sf MiddleEdges}
and
{\sf CrossingEdges}
.

First, we label all endpoints of all the edges of
{\sf MiddleEdges}
with the label
{\cal M}
.

Next, we guess two sets of edges
{\sf cross}({\sf lsep}),{\sf cross}({\sf rsep})
of size at most
\sqrt{k}
that cross
{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}
, respectively. Then, we define
{\sf CrossingEdges}\coloneqq{\sf cross}({\sf lsep})\cup{\sf cross}({\sf rsep})
. Note that

|{\sf CrossingEdges}|\leq 2\sqrt{k}

and the number of guesses is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.

We now describe feasible pairs for labels of the endpoints of
{\sf CrossingEdges}
; and each guess corresponds to using labeling the endpoint of each edge in
{\sf CrossingEdges}
by one of the feasible (ordered) pairs. However, if the guess for the label is
incompatible
with a previously assigned label, then we consider it as an invalid guess and proceed to the next one. Note that, if a vertex has previously been assigned multiple labels, then the incompatible label(s) is a label that has not been assigned to the said vertex, if any.

•

For
e\in{\sf cross}({\sf lsep})
,
the feasible label pairs are
({\cal L},{\cal M}),({\cal M},{\cal L}),({\cal L},{\cal R}),({\cal R},{\cal L})
.

•

For
e\in{\sf cross}({\sf rsep})
,
the feasible label pairs are
({\cal R},{\cal M}),({\cal M},{\cal R}),({\cal L},{\cal R}),({\cal R},{\cal L})
.

Overall, we have at most
\binom{m}{2\sqrt{k}}^{4}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices. Let
{\sf CrossLeft,CrossRight}\subseteq{\sf Endpoints}({\sf CrossingEdges})
denote the subsets of vertices that receive labels
{\cal L},{\cal R}
, respectively.

Step D: Labeling
{\sf comps}(v)
for
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})

Recall that for each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, each component
C\in{\sf comps}(v)
can be classified into
{\sf Special}(v),\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v),{\sf UpDown}(v),
and
{\sf Pendants}(v)
according to
Definition

10
. Now we describe how to handle each of the components separately.

\blacktriangleright

{\sf Special}(v)
.

Some of the vertices in such components are already labeled. We will return to the remaining vertices in these components at a later step.

\blacktriangleright

\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
.

Recall that each
\pi_{i}
for
i\in[2]
is comprised of
\lambda=\mathcal{O}(\log k^{\star})
total orders
\pi_{i}^{1},\pi_{i}^{2},\ldots,\pi_{i}^{\lambda}
for
V_{i}^{1},V_{i}^{2},\ldots,V_{i}^{\lambda}
, respectively. For each
i\in[2]
, and for each
j\in[\lambda]
, we do the following.

•

We guess two vertices
u(j,l),w(j,r)\in V^{j}_{i}
with
\pi^{j}_{i}(u(j,l))<\pi^{j}_{i}(w(j,r))
.

•

Any
C\in\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
that contains a vertex
u\in V^{j}_{i}
with
\pi^{j}_{i}(u)\leq\pi^{j}_{i}(u(j,l))
, we label all the vertices of
C
using
{\cal L}
.

•

Any
C\in\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
that contains a vertex
w\in V^{j}_{i}
with
\pi^{j}_{i}(w)\geq\pi^{j}_{i}(w(j,r))
, we label all the vertices of
C
using
{\cal R}
.

If any of the guesses made in this step is incompatible with a previous step, or with a prior guess made in this step, then we terminate this guess. Note that the number of guesses made in this step is at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\lambda)}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\log k^{\star})}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
, since
k=\Omega(\sqrt{k^{\star}})
.

\blacktriangleright

{\sf UpDown}(v)
.

By
Lemma

3
, we know that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 8\sqrt{k}
for each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
. Thus, if the number of such components is more than
8\sqrt{k}
, then we terminate the guess. Otherwise, we guess the label of each of them. The number of guesses for this is bounded by
\binom{n}{8\sqrt{k}}\cdot 2^{8\sqrt{k}}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.

\blacktriangleright

{\sf Pendants}(v)
.

We distinguish between
DE, SB, ST cases
and use
Lemma

4
to label components as applicable. In
DE
case, we label all the components in
\bigcup_{v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})}{\sf Pendants}(v)
. However, in
SB case
(resp.
ST case
), we are left with labeling
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))
(resp.
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
), which we do next.

We focus on the
SB
case and show how in accordance with
Definition

12
and
Lemma

5
. The
ST
case is handled analogously. Let
v\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2)
, and let
{\sf TotalNum}
denote the total sum of the multiplicities of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
. First, we guess a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}=({\sf LeftPNum},{\sf RightPNum})
, for which there are at most
n
possibilities. Then, for each choice of
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
, we use a knapsack-style dynamic programming to find a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compatible
{\sf PendantGuess}=({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
in polynomial time, if one exists. If such a partition does not exist, then we move to the next guess. Otherwise, we label the vertices of
{\sf LeftPendants}
using
{\cal L}
and that of
{\sf RightPendants}
using
{\cal R}
.

Step E: Label propagation.

Let
A
denote the subset of vertices that have already received a label, and let
A^{\cal L},A^{\cal R}\subseteq A
denote the subsets of
A
that have received the respective label. Further, let
O\subseteq A
denote the subset of vertices that have received a unique label, and again let
O^{\cal L},O^{\cal R}\subseteq O
denote the partition of
O
into two disjoint subsets. At this point, some of the vertices of the graph may still be unlabeled, which we label during this phase. To this end, we consider normal (
\blacklozenge
) and elaborate (
\clubsuit
) instances separately.

\blacklozenge
Define the following sets:

\displaystyle{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}

\displaystyle={\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf leftbound},{\sf lsep}\right\})\cup{\sf CrossLeft}

\displaystyle{\sf Origin}^{\cal R}

\displaystyle={\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf rightbound},{\sf rsep}\right\})\cup{\sf CrossRight}

\clubsuit
Define the following sets:

\displaystyle{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}

\displaystyle=({\sf LeftExtBnd}\cap A^{\cal L})\cup{\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf leftbound},{\sf lsep}\right\})\cup{\sf CrossLeft}

\displaystyle{\sf Origin}^{\cal R}

\displaystyle=({\sf RightExtBnd}\cap A^{\cal R})\cup{\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf rightbound},{\sf rsep}\right\})\cup{\sf CrossRight}

Now, regardless of
\blacklozenge
or
\clubsuit
, we proceed as follows.

1.

For each
u\in{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
, and each unlabeled
w\not\in A
that is reachable from
u
in the graph
G-(O^{\cal M}\cup O^{\cal R})
, we label it using
{\cal L}
.

2.

For each
u\in{\sf Origin}^{\cal R}
, and each unlabeled
w\not\in A
that is reachable from
u
in the graph
G-(O^{\cal L}\cup O^{\cal M})
, we label it using
{\cal R}
.

3.

For each
u\in{\sf Origin}^{\cal M}
, and each unlabeled
w\not\in A
that is reachable from
u
in the graph
G-(O^{\cal L}\cup O^{\cal R})
, we label it using
{\cal M}
.

During this process, if a previously unlabeled vertex, i.e.,
w\notin A
receives multiple labels, then we terminate the guess.

Otherwise, for
{\cal T}\in\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
, let
V^{\cal T}
denote the subset of vertices that have received the respective label.

Step F: Partial Order.

Recall that we are given a partial orders
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
. For each
i\in[2]
, we will guess permutation between certain subsets of vertices, which will be used to extend
\pi_{i}
to a new partial order
\pi^{\prime}_{i}
.

Let
S\coloneqq{\sf Mid}_{1}\cup{\sf Mid}_{2}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf CrossingEdges})
. The bounds on the respective sets imply that
|S|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
. For each
i\in[2]
, let
S_{i}\coloneqq V_{i}\cap S
. We guess permutations
\pi^{\lambda+1}_{i}
of
S_{i}
for each
i\in[2]
. Since
|S_{i}|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
, it follows that the total number of guesses is
|S_{1}|!\cdot|S_{2}|!=(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})!)^{2}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k}\log k)}
. Let
\pi^{\lambda+1}=(\pi^{\lambda+1}_{1},\pi^{\lambda+1}_{2})
. We say that
\pi^{\lambda+1}
is
valid
if the following condition is met.

•

\clubsuit
For each
i\in[2]
, for any distinct
u,v\in S_{i}
, if
\pi^{\lambda+1}_{i}(u)<\pi^{\lambda+1}_{i}(v)
, then, either
u
and
v
are incomparable in
\pi_{i}
, or
\pi_{i}(u)<\pi_{i}(v)
.

•

\pi^{\lambda+1}
satisfies the following properties (that must be satisfied by the separator):

–

{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}
do not cross

–

For each
e\in{\sf Sep}
, the set of edges crossing
e
is exactly equal to
{\sf cross}(e)
,

–

{\sf MiddleEdges}
is exactly the set of edges that lie between
{\sf lsep},{\sf rsep}

Note that the validity of
\pi^{\lambda+1}
can be checked in polynomial time. If a guess for
\pi^{\lambda+1}_{i}
is invalid, then we terminate it and move to the next guess.

\clubsuit
We define
\pi^{\lambda+2}
as an ordering over
{\sf ExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)
by combining parts of
\pi^{\lambda+1}_{1}
, and one of the constituent total orders of
\pi^{2}
that is a permutation of the set
{\sf ExtBnd}
. Then, we define
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{1}\cup\pi^{\lambda+1}_{1}\cup\pi^{\lambda+2}_{1}
, and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq\pi_{2}\cup\pi^{\lambda+1}_{2}
. Note that
\pi^{\prime}_{i}
is a valid extension of
\pi_{i}
, since
\pi^{\lambda+1}
is valid. Henceforth, we assume that we are working with such a
\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})
for some guess for
\pi^{\lambda+1}
.

Step G: Parameter division.

Let
k_{\sf current}
denote the number of crossings among the edges of
{\sf CrossingEdges}\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}\cup{\sf Sep}
according to
\zeta
. If
k_{\sf current}>k
, then we terminate the current guess. Otherwise, let
k^{\prime}\coloneqq k-k_{\sf current}
. We guess two non-negative integers
0\leq k^{\cal L},k^{\cal R}\leq k/2
such that
k^{\prime}=k^{\cal L}+k^{\cal R}
. Note that the number of such guesses is bounded by
k^{2}
.

The following lemma follows from the description of Step 2.

Lemma 6
.

The total number of guesses made in steps A through G, for (i) separator
{\sf Sep}
and its associated sets of vertices and edges, (ii) labels of vertices of
V(G)
, and (iii) division of
k
into smaller parameters, is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.

4.4
Step 3: Creating sub-instances.

For each set of guesses made in the previous step (as stated in
Lemma

6
), we create a pair of sub-instances, a left sub-instance
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L}
, and a right sub-instance
\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
. In this step, we construct each such pair of sub-instances corresponding to a particular guess. Then, in the next step, we will recursively call our algorithm to solve each of the two sub-instances.

Creating left sub-instance.

Let
G^{{\cal L}}=G[V^{{\cal L}}]
, where recall that
V^{\cal L}
is a set of all the vertices with label
{\cal L}
(including a vertex with multiple labels). Next, we add certain multi-edges in the graph.

Creating mutli-edges.
For each edge
uv\in{\sf cross}({\sf lsep})
, we identify a multi-edge
e_{add}(uv)
to be added in each case. After the case analysis, we will discuss the multiplicity of this edge, and actually add it to the graph.

•

u\in V_{1}
has label
{\cal L}
and
v\in V_{2}
has label has label
{\cal M}
or
{\cal R}
.

Operation.
Define
e_{add}(uv)\coloneqq(u,{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))
.

•

v\in V_{2}
has label
{\cal L}
, and
u\in V_{1}
has label
{\cal M}
or
{\cal R}
.

Operation.
Define
e_{add}(uv)\coloneqq({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1),v)
.

Now, we proceed as follows:

•

If an edge is defined as
e_{add}
of multiple edges, say
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
, then its multiplicity is defined as the sum of the respective multiplicities of
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
– here we adopt the convention that, if an edge
e_{i}
belongs to
{\sf RegularEdges}
, then its multiplicity is
1
.

•

Finally, we add
e_{add}
with the combined multiplicity to
{\sf WeightedEdges}^{\cal L}
. Here, if
e_{add}
is already in
{\sf RegularEdges}
, we do not remove it.

Let
G^{{\cal L}}
denote the resulting graph. The left sub-instance is an extended instance
\mathcal{I}^{{\cal L}}=(G^{{\cal L}},V_{1}^{{\cal L}},V_{2}^{{\cal L}},{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal L},\pi^{\cal L},k^{\cal L})
, where:

•

For each
i\in[2]
,
V_{i}^{\cal L}\coloneqq V_{i}\cap V^{\cal L}
, and
\pi_{i}^{\cal L}=\pi^{\prime}_{i}
projected to
V^{\cal L}
.

•

{\sf BoundaryEdges}^{\cal L}=\left\{{\sf leftbound}^{\cal L},{\sf rightbound}^{\cal L}\right\}
, where
{\sf leftbound}^{\cal L}={\sf leftbound}
,
{\sf rightbound}^{\cal L}={\sf lsep}
.

•

\blacklozenge
Let
{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal L}={\sf LeftExtBnd}^{\cal L}={\sf RightExtBnd}^{\cal L}=\emptyset
, since these sets are irrelevant for normal extended instances.

\clubsuit
For elaborate extended instances, let
{\sf LeftExtBnd}^{\cal L}={\sf LeftExtBnd}\cap V^{\cal L}
and
{\sf RightExtBnd}^{\cal L}={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1)
, and
{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal L}={\sf LeftExtBnd}^{\cal L}\cup{\sf RightExtBnd}^{\cal L}
.

At this point, we perform the following check based on whether
\mathcal{I}
was a normal (
\blacklozenge
), or an elaborate (
\clubsuit
) extended instance.

\blacklozenge
If
G^{\cal L}
is not connected, then we terminate the guess.

\clubsuit
If each
u\in V^{\cal L}
is reachable from some vertex of
v\in{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal L}
in
G^{\cal L}
, then we terminate the guess.

The right sub-instance
\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}=(G^{{\cal R}},V_{1}^{{\cal R}},V_{2}^{{\cal R}},{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal R},\pi^{\cal R},k^{\cal R})
is created analogously, and we perform the connectivity check as described above; we omit the description.

Further, we note the following auxiliary observations, that satisfy the constraints on the extended instances, as mentioned in
Definition

2
.

•

\clubsuit
The parameter in each recursive call reduces by at least a factor of
1/2
(and the base case is triggered when
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
), and each time we add two total orders to the relation
\pi
, it follows that the total number of partial orders remains bounded by
4\log k^{\star}
at each step as required in
Item

4
.

•

\clubsuit
The size of
{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal L},{\sf ExtBnd}^{\cal R}
remains bounded by
{\sf ExtBnd}
, which was originally
3\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, satisfying
Item

3
.

•

Note that, in the original instance
{\sf WeightedEdges}=\emptyset
, and in each call of the algorithm, the total sum of multiplicities of
{\sf WeightedEdges}
incident to a vertex
u={\sf endpt}(e,i)
for some
e\in{\sf Sep}
, and some
i\in[2]
added in that call, is at most the number of crossings the corresponding separator edge
e
is involved in–which is bounded by
\sqrt{k}
. Again, since the parameter decreases by a constant factor in each recursive call, it holds that the total sum of multiplicities of all edges incident to a vertex, remains bounded by
\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\sqrt{(1/2)^{\ell}\cdot k^{\star}}=2\sqrt{k^{\star}}
.
Item

6

From the previous discussion, the following claim follows.

Claim 4.1
.

If
\mathcal{I}
was a valid normal (resp. elaborate) extended instance, then
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
are valid normal (resp. elaborate) extended instances.

4.5
Step 4: Conquer left and right.

For each guess in Step 2, we obtain a pair of sub-instances
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
, with each having a parameter at most
k/2
. We focus on one pair of instances that we solve recursively in time
2\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
. There are two possibilities. The first one is that at least one of the recursive calls returns that the corresponding sub-instance is a
No
-instance, in which case we proceed to the next guess. Hence, assume that both recursive calls return that
\mathcal{I}^{{\cal L}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{{\cal R}}
are
Yes
-instances, and in addition they also output an ordering of the corresponding vertices.

4.6
Step 5: Obtaining the Final Drawing.

If, for any of the non-terminated guesses, all three recursive calls reporting that the respective sub-instances are
Yes
-instances, along with the corresponding orderings, then we can combine these orderings to obtain orderings of
V_{1},V_{2}
that has at most
k
crossings, and respect the given partial order
\pi
.

Specifically, suppose
\sigma^{\cal L},\sigma^{\cal R}
be the drawings of
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
returned by the corresponding recursive calls. Then, we obtain the final drawing of
\mathcal{I}
by defining
\varsigma^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\sigma_{i}^{\cal L}\circ\pi^{\prime}_{i}({\sf Mid}_{i})\circ\sigma_{i}^{\cal R}
for
i\in[2]
. We return
\sigma^{\prime}=(\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{2})
as our final drawing. Otherwise, if for each guess, either (i) it is terminated due to incompatibility, or (ii) one of the three sub-instances corresponding to the guess is found to be a
No
-instance, then we output that
\mathcal{I}
is a
No
-instance.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
be a pair of sub-instances (with parameters
k^{\cal L},k^{\cal R}
) corresponding to some guess made in Step 2. Further, let
\sigma^{\cal L},\sigma^{\cal R}
be valid drawings of the
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
. Then, if we obtain a combined drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
as in Step 5, then
\sigma^{\prime}
is a valid drawing of
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

Recall that, in step F we guess the permutations
\pi^{\lambda+1}_{i}
of
S_{i}
, and we use it in step G to determine the number of crossings
k_{\sf current}
among the edges of
{\sf CrossingEdges}\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}\cup{\sf Sep}
. Then, we define
k=k^{\prime}+k_{\sf current}
, and divide it into
k^{\cal L}+k^{\cal R}
, which are used as parameters for the respective sub-instances.

Next, we claim that all the
k_{\sf current}
crossings among the edges are not counted in any of the sub-instances. To this end, first observe that the edges of
{\sf MiddleEdges}
are not involved in any of the subproblems. Next, consider any pair of edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\sf CrossingEdges}\cup{\sf Sep}
, such that the crossing between
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
is counted in
k_{\sf current}
. Note that both
e_{1},e_{2}
cannot be in
{\sf Sep}
, since the guessed permutation must satisfy that these edges do not cross. Then, suppose
e_{1}\in{\sf Sep}
and
e_{2}\in{\sf CrossingEdges}
. Then in each of the (at most) two sub-instances, the edge
e_{2}
is replaced by a multi-edge
e^{\prime}_{2}
incident to one of the endpoints of
e
. Thus, in any drawing of the respective sub-instance, the edge
e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
cannot cross.
11
11

11

Note that in different sub-instances, the multi-edge
e^{\prime}_{2}
that replaces
e_{2}
is different; however, in each of the sub-instances, it holds that
e_{1}
cannot cross with any of the replacing
e^{\prime}_{2}
.

Finally, consider the case when
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\sf CrossingEdges}
. Since
e_{1},e_{2}
cross, we have two cases: (a)
e_{1}\in{\sf cross}(e^{\prime}_{1})
and
e_{2}\in{\sf cross}(e^{\prime}_{2})
for distinct edges
e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2}\in{\sf Sep}
, or (b)
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\sf cross}(e)
for some
e\in{\sf Sep}
. In case (a), the multi-edges replacing
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
belong to completely different sub-instances (note that we do not have a middle sub-instance). Thus, we are left with case (b), when they cross the same edge in the separator.
Then, in each of the corresponding sub-instance, there is a multi-edge
e^{\prime}_{1}
that replaces
e_{1}
, and a multi-edge
e^{\prime}_{2}
that replaces
e_{2}
. If
e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
are incident to the same endpoint of
e
, then they cannot cross. Alternatively, if
e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
are incident to different endpoints of
e
, then due to
Footnote

5
in
Definition

3
, we do not count such a crossing. In any case, we have that the crossing between
e,e^{\prime}\in{\sf CrossingEdges}\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}\cup{\sf Sep}
is not counted in any of the sub-instances.

Next, we note that in
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L}
, the left boundary edge
{\sf leftbound}
(and left extended boundary
{\sf LeftExtBnd}
, in the case of
\clubsuit
elaborate instance) is derived from the current instance
\mathcal{I}
, and thus are placed at the appropriate locations. An analogous property holds about
\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
. Next, we note that the orderings
\pi^{\cal L},\pi^{\cal R}
is obtained by the projecting
\pi^{\prime}
(which is an extension of
\pi
) to the respective vertex subsets. Since the respective drawings are compatible with these orders, it follows that the combined drawing is compatible with
\pi^{\prime}
, and hence with
\pi
. Furthermore, since
\pi^{\prime}
also contains the orderings
\pi^{\lambda+1}
, the respective drawings also satisfy the ordering on the vertices of
{\sf LeftExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)
.

Finally, since
\sigma^{\cal L},\sigma^{\cal R}
are valid drawings for
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
respectively, when we obtain the combined drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
by gluing the three drawings, it is straightforward to verify that
Definition

3
are satisfied. This shows that
\sigma^{\prime}
is a valid drawing for
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings.
∎

4.7
Proof of Correctness

Lemma 8
.

\mathcal{I}
is an
Yes
-instance for parameter
k
iff the algorithm returns a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{I}
with at most
k
crossings.

Proof.

In the base case when
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
,
the correctness of the algorithm follows via
Lemma

1
. Thus, we consider the recursive case. Suppose inductively that the statement is true for all values of parameter strictly smaller than
k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, and we want to show the statement holds for
k
.

Forward direction.
Suppose
\mathcal{I}
is a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, and let
\sigma
be a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Consider the guess where the following objects are correctly guessed in the algorithm:

1.

Separator
{\sf Sep}(\sigma)
as guaranteed by
Lemma

2
,

2.

Sets
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})
and
{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
and the relative ordering between the corresponding vertices is guessed according to
\sigma
.

3.

For each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, by
Lemma

3
,
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 4\sqrt{k}
. Then, for each component
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
, if all the vertices of
C\cap V_{i}
belong to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},i)
(resp. to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},i)
) for each
i\in[2]
, then consider the guess where it is labeled
{\cal L}
(resp.
{\cal R}
).

Now we distinguish between three cases, namely
DT, SB, ST
.

\blacktriangleright

DE case.

Here, all components of
{\sf Pendants}(v),v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
behave according to
Lemma

4
, which is how the algorithm labels the respective components.

\blacktriangleright

SB case.

In this case, all components of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},2))\cup{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep},2))
behave according to
Lemma

4
, and the algorithm also labels such components accordingly.

Now, we are left with labeling the components of
{\sf Pendants}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1))
. In this case, let
({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum})
be the pair of integers guaranteed by
Lemma

5
, and consider the guess corresponding to
({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum})
. In this case,
Lemma

5
implies that a
({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum})
-partition of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
exists, and one such partition, say
({\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
will be found by the algorithm.
Lemma

5
implies that there exists a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
in the family
{\sf NiceFamily}(\sigma,{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep},1),{\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum},{\sf LeftPendants},{\sf RightPendants})
, w.r.t. which
{\sf Sep}(\sigma)
is still a separator, and furthermore,
\sigma^{\prime}
is compatible with all the guesses made so far. Henceforth, we consider this guess, and use
\sigma^{\prime}
instead of
\sigma
in the subsequent analysis. For ease of notation, let us rename
\sigma\leftarrow\sigma^{\prime}
.

\blacktriangleright

ST case
.

This case is handled in a symmetric manner as
SB
case, and we omit the description.

Let
\sigma
be the drawing obtained at the end after appropriate redefintion (if any), as described above. In the following claim, we show that the labeling process correctly labels all the vertices w.r.t. the (new) drawing
\sigma
.

Claim 4.2
.

Let
\sigma
be the (new) drawing as described above, and consider the guess as defined above. Then, after the vertex labeling process of step H corresponding to this particular guess, we have that
V^{\cal T}=V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})
, for each
{\cal T}\in\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
. Note that the definition of the sets
V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})
can be found in
Definition

9
.

Proof of
4.2
.

We consider
\clubsuit
elaborate instances first, since the proof for the
\blacklozenge
normal instances is only simpler.

Let us consider a vertex
u\in V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})
. By of
Definition

14
,
u
is reachable from some vertex
v\in{\sf ExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
, say via a path
P(uv)
.
We consider different cases based on
v
and a path
P(u\leadsto v)
. Let
P(uv)=\langle u=w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{\ell}=v\rangle
. Let
w_{i+1}
be the first vertex (if any) along
P(u\leadsto v)
, such
w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{i}\in V(\sigma,{\cal L})
, and
w_{i+1}\not\in V(\sigma,{\cal L})
. Otherwise, let
w_{i}=w_{\ell}=v
. We consider these two cases separately; see
Figure

16
.

Figure 16:
Illustration of the two cases in
4.2
. In case (i) on the left, the entire path contains vertices of
V(\sigma,{\cal L})
, and the path ends at vertex
v\in{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
. In case (ii) on the right, the path goes outside
V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})
; however to do so, it must contain a crossing edge (shown as dashed red), in which case its left endpoint
w_{i}\in{\sf CrossLeft}\subseteq{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
.

(i) If
w_{i}=v
, then since
w_{i}\in V(\sigma,{\cal L})
, it cannot be the case that
w_{i}\in({\sf RightExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf rightbound}))\setminus{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
. Thus, it implies that
w_{i}\in{\sf LeftExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf leftbound})\cup{\sf CrossLeft}={\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
.

(ii) If
w_{i}\neq v
, then we know that
w_{i+1}
, the vertex immediately after
w_{i}
in
P(uv)
satisfies that
w_{i+1}\notin V(\sigma,{\cal L})
. Then, it follows that the edge
(w_{i},w_{i+1})
crosses some edge of
{\sf Sep}
–implying that
w_{i}\in{\sf CrossLeft}
.

This implies that, for any vertex
u\in V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})\setminus{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
, and any path
P(u\leadsto v)
to a vertex
v\in{\sf ExtBnd}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
, the last vertex
w
with label
{\cal L}
must belong to the set
{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
. Further, the subpath
P(u\leadsto w)
does not contain any vertex with a label
{\cal M}
or
{\cal R}
. This also implies that, all the vertices of
V(\sigma,{\cal L})
are reachable from some vertex
w^{\prime}\in{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
in the graph
G-(O^{{\cal M}}\cup O^{{\cal R}})
. Thus, all the unlabeled vertices in
V(\sigma,{\cal L})
get labeled
{\cal L}
in the label propagation phase. This shows that
V(\sigma,{\cal L})\subseteq V^{\cal L}
. Similar proofs also show that
V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})\subseteq V^{\cal M}
, and
V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})\subseteq V^{\cal R}
. Now, consider a vertex of
u^{\prime}\in(V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})\cup V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R}))\setminus{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
. However, such a vertex
u^{\prime}
belongs to
O^{\cal M}\cup O^{\cal R}
, and hence is not part of the graph
G-(O^{\cal M}\cup O^{\cal R})
. Thus, it cannot receive label
{\cal L}
, which shows that
V^{\cal L}\subseteq V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})
. By combining both containments, we obtain that
V^{\cal L}=V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})
. Similar proofs also show that
V^{\cal M}=V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})
, and
V^{\cal R}=V(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})
, and are therefore omitted.

The proof for
\blacklozenge
normal instances is simpler, since the graph
G
is connected. Therefore, each vertex
u
is reachable from any other vertex, and we can select an arbitrary vertex to play the part of
v
in the proof above.
∎

After accounting for the
k_{\sf current}
crossings among the endpoints of
{\sf CrossingEdges}\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}\cup{\sf Sep}
in
\sigma
, the rest of the
k^{\prime}
crossings are either to the left of
{\sf lsep}
or to the right of
{\sf rsep}
(this argument is similar to
Lemma

7
). Let the number of these crossings be
k^{\cal L},k^{\cal M}
, respectively, and consider the guess where these numbers are correctly guessed. Further, since we label all the vertices correctly, it follows that all the vertices of
V^{\cal L}
(resp.
V^{\cal R}
) are reachable from
{\sf Origin}^{\cal L}
(resp.
{\sf Origin}^{\cal R}
).

\blacklozenge
For normal extended instances, we note that any
u\leadsto v
path, where
u,v\in V^{\cal L}
, must either be completely contained inside
G^{\prime{\cal L}}=G[V^{\cal L}]
, use an edge of
{\sf cross}({\sf lsep})\cup{\sf lsep}
. In any case, due to the way we add extra edges to
{\sf WeightedEdges}^{\cal L}
, it follows that
u
and
v
are also reachable in
G^{\cal L}
.

\clubsuit
Now, consider elaborate extended instances. Then, we observe that vertices of
{\sf CrossLeft}
(resp.
{\sf CrossRight}
) are directly connected to one of the vertices of
{\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf lsep}\right\})
(resp.
{\sf Endpoints}(\left\{{\sf rsep}\right\})
) in the graph
G^{\cal L}
(resp.
G^{\cal R}
).

In either case, we have shown that these guesses are not terminated by the connectivity check done in Step 3 after creating the instances
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
, respectively. Then,
4.1
implies that
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
are valid extended instances.
Further,
\sigma
restricted to the respective vertex sets
V^{\cal L},V^{\cal R}
gives a valid drawing of the respective instances. This implies that
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L},\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
are
Yes
-instances for the parameters
k^{\cal L},k^{\cal R}
, respectively.

Next, we claim that, since
\mathcal{I}
is a
k
-minimal instance, both of these instances must also be minimal instances for the respective parameters. suppose for contradiction that
\mathcal{I}^{\cal L}
is a
Yes
-instance, but is not
k^{\cal L}
-minimal
Yes
-instance, then it admits a drawing
(\sigma^{\prime})^{\cal L}
with
(k^{\prime})^{\cal L}<k^{\cal L}
crossings. Then, we can apply an argument from
Lemma

7
, in order to obtain a drawing for
\mathcal{I}
with fewer than
k
crossings, contradicting the
k
-minimality of
\mathcal{I}
. A similar argument also holds for
\mathcal{I}^{\cal R}
.

Then, by induction (since
k^{\cal L},k^{\cal R}\leq k/2
) the respective recursive calls return valid drawings
\sigma^{\cal L},\sigma^{\cal R}
of the respective instances. Then,
Lemma

7
implies that that in Step 5, we return a combined drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
corresponding to this guess.

Reverse direction.
The reverse direction is trivial, since the valid drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{I}
witnesses that
\mathcal{I}
is a
Yes
-instance.

∎

In the following lemma, we argue that the algorithm runs in subexponential time.

Lemma 9
.

For any input extended instance
\mathcal{I}
with parameter
k
, the algorithm runs in time
T_{2}(k)\leq n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.

Proof.

The base case corresponds to when
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
,
for some constant
c
. In this case,
Lemma

1
implies that the algorithm runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.

Otherwise, consider the recursive case, and suppose the claim is true for all
k^{\prime}\leq k-1
, where
k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
is the parameter of the given instance
\mathcal{I}
.
Lemma

6
implies that the total number of guesses is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
, and corresponding to each guess that is not terminated, we make two recursive calls to the algorithm with parameters at most
k/2
each. Thus,
T_{2}(n,k)
satisfies the recurrence given in
Equation

1
. Then, the claim follows via induction.
∎

By combining
2
,
Lemma

8
,
Lemma

9
, and the fact that due to linear-time kernelization algorithm of
Proposition

3
, we have
n=(k^{\star})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, we obtain the following theorem (where we rename
k^{\star}
as
k
).

Theorem 6
.

On an
n
vertex graph,
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k}\log k)}+n\cdot k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, where
k
denotes the number of crossings.

5
Subexponential Algorithm for
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization

In this section, we will design our subexponential FPT algorithm for
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
running in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2/3}\log k)}+nk^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. The overall structure of this algorithm is similar to that for
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
– the algorithm is recursive, and is based on dividing the instance into multiple sub-instances with the help of a separator (consisting of at most 4 edges, and two special subsets of vertices) with at most
\alpha k
crossings its either side. However, the details are different in the following two crucial aspects: (i) the structure of the separator is more complex, which necessitates handling multiple cases (like
DE, SB, ST cases
for
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
), and (ii) the analysis of connected components in
G-v
, where
v
belongs to one of the endpoints of the separator edges, is more involved. Recall that, in the case of two layers, we needed to handle the set of pendant vertices attached to
v
, where we showed the existence of another, more structured, separator-compatible drawing in
Lemma

5
. For three layers, this requires a much more sophisticated analysis. Another notable aspect we would like to highlight is that, at various places in the algorithm for three layers, we will use the two layer algorithm as a subroutine.

5.1
Setting Up

First, we adopt the same notation set up in
Definition

1
for
{\sf endpt}(\cdot),{\sf Endpoints}(\cdot)
and extend it to three layers. Further, in any (extended) instance, we partition the set of edges into
E_{12}\cup E_{23}
, where
E_{12}
(resp.
E_{23}
) is the set of edges with one endpoint in
V_{1}
and another in
V_{2}
(resp. one endpoint in
V_{2}
and another in
V_{3}
).
Next, we describe the notion of an extended instance.

We denote the
original
instance of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
as
(H,U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},k^{\star})
. In the course of the recursive algorithm, our algorithm will produce
extended instances
of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
, defined as follows.

Definition 14

(Extended Instance for
h=3
)
.

An
extended instance
of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
is given by
(G,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},{\sf ExtBnd},\pi,k_{12},k_{23},k)
where,

1.

V(G)=V_{1}\uplus V_{2}\uplus V_{3}
,

2.

E(G)={\sf RegularEdges}\uplus{\sf WeightedEdges}\uplus{\sf BoundaryEdges}
.

The set of edges between
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
respectively are denoted by
E_{12}
, and those between
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
are denoted
E_{23}
.

3.

{\sf ExtBnd}=({\sf LeftExtBnd},{\sf RightExtBnd})
, where

•

{\sf BoundaryEdges}=\left\{{\sf leftbound}_{12},{\sf rightbound}_{12},{\sf leftbound}_{23},{\sf rightbound}_{23}\right\}
is a set of at most four
boundary edges
of the instance, where
{\sf leftbound}_{12},{\sf rightbound}_{12}\in E_{12}
(both may be equal), and
{\sf leftbound}_{23},{\sf rightbound}_{23}\in E_{23}
(both may be equal).

•

{\sf LeftExtBnd},{\sf RightExtBnd}\subseteq V_{2}
is a set of at most
3\sqrt{k^{\star}}
vertices, where

(i)
{\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{12},2),{\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{23},2)\in{\sf LeftExtBnd}
, and

(ii)
{\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{12},2),{\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{23},2)\in{\sf RightExtBnd}
.

•

Further, each
v\in V(G)
is reachable from some vertex in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})\cup{\sf LeftExtBnd}\cup{\sf RightExtBnd}
.

4.

\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3})
, where for each
i\in[3]
,
\pi_{i}
is a partial order on
V_{i}
such that,
\pi_{i}
is the union of
\lambda\geq 0
different relations
\pi_{i}^{1},\pi_{i}^{2},\ldots,\pi_{i}^{\lambda}
, where for each
1\leq j\leq\lambda
,
\pi_{i}^{j}
is a total order on
V_{i}^{j}\subseteq V_{i}
, and the sets
\left\{V_{i}^{j}:1\leq j\leq\lambda\right\}
are not necessarily disjoint.
12
12

12

The fact that each
\pi_{i}
is a union of
\lambda
total orders is crucial only at a few specific steps in the algorithm, which is where we will make this notation explicit.
Here,
\lambda
always remains bounded by
4\log(k^{\star})
.

One of the orderings
\pi^{j}_{2}
has ground set
V^{j}_{2}={\sf ExtBnd}
, and it orders these vertices in the following way from left to right:

u^{l}_{1}<
vertices of
{\sf LeftExtBnd}\setminus\left\{u^{l}_{1},u^{l}_{2}\right\}
in some order
<u^{l}_{2}<u^{r}_{1}<
vertices of
{\sf RightExtBnd}\setminus\left\{u^{r}_{1},u^{r}_{2}\right\}
in some order
<u^{r}_{2}
. Here,
\left\{u^{l}_{1},u^{l}_{2}\right\}=\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{12},2),{\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{23},2)\right\}
, and
\left\{u^{r}_{1},u^{r}_{2}\right\}=\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{12},2),{\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{23},2)\right\}
.

5.

Each
e\in{\sf WeightedEdges}
is referred to as a
multi-edge
, and has an associated weight/multiplicity
\mu(e)
(which is a non-negative integer).

6.

Each multi-edge
e\in{\sf WeightedEdges}
is incident to exactly one vertex of
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
. Further, for each vertex
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
, the total sum of multiplicities of multi-edges incident to
v
is at most
2\sqrt{k^{\star}}
.

7.

k=k_{12}+k_{23}
, where
k,k_{12},k_{23}
are non-negative integers.

Figure 17:
Left: Drawing of an extended instance for
h=3
.
{\sf BoundaryEdges}
are shown as dashed magenta edges. Note that the top right and top right boundary edges share an endpoint.
WeightedEdges
(shown in orange) are incident only to
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf BoundaryEdges})
(shown as unfilled nodes). As explained in
Definition

15
, crossings between multi-edges
ad
and
bc
are counted with multiplicities (
2\times 3=6
), however, those between
ae
and
pq
are counted as
0
, since these multi-edges are incident to different endpoints of the same boundary edge, namely
{\sf leftbound}_{23}
.

Next, we define the notion of a drawing of an extended instance, along the same lines as
Definition

3
.

Definition 15

(Drawing of an extended instance.)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(G,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},\pi,k_{12},k_{23},k)
be an extended instance of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
. We say that
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
is a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
if the following properties are satisfied.

1.

\sigma_{i}
is a permutation of
V_{i}
for each
i\in[3]
.

2.

Ordering of vertices on respective lines:

(a)

\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{12},1))\leq\sigma_{1}(u_{1})\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{12},1))
for all
u_{1}\in V_{1}
.

(b)

Let
\ell_{12}={\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{12},2),\ell_{23}={\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{23},2)
,

r_{12}={\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{12},2)
, and
r_{23}={\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{23},2)
. Then,

•

\min\left\{\sigma_{2}(\ell_{12}),\sigma_{2}(\ell_{23})\right\}\leq\sigma_{2}(u^{l})\leq\max\left\{\sigma_{2}(\ell_{12}),\sigma_{2}(\ell_{23})\right\}
for all
u^{l}\in{\sf LeftExtBnd}
,

•

\min\left\{\sigma_{2}(r_{12}),\sigma_{2}(r_{23})\right\}\leq\sigma_{2}(u^{r})\leq\max\left\{\sigma_{2}(r_{12}),\sigma_{2}(r_{23})\right\}
for all
u^{r}\in{\sf RightExtBnd}
,

•
