\min\left\{\sigma_{2}(\ell_{12}),\sigma_{2}(\ell_{23})\right\}\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\max\left\{\sigma_{2}(r_{12}),\sigma_{2}(r_{23})\right\}
for all
u\in V_{2}
.

(c)

\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{23},3))\leq\sigma_{3}(u_{3})\leq\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{23},3))
for all
u_{3}\in V_{3}
.

(d)

For each
e\in E_{12}
, for each
i\in[2]
,
\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{12},i))\leq\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}(e,i))\leq\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{12},i))
.

(e)

For each
e\in E_{23}
, for each
j\in\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\sigma_{j}({\sf endpt}({\sf leftbound}_{23},j))\leq\sigma_{j}({\sf endpt}(e,j))\leq\sigma_{j}({\sf endpt}({\sf rightbound}_{23},j))
.

3.

For each
u,v\in V_{i}
, if
\pi_{i}(u)<\pi_{i}(v)
, then
\sigma_{i}(u)<\sigma_{i}(v)
, i.e.,
\sigma_{i}
is compatible with
\pi_{i}
.

Further, we count the crossings in the drawing
\sigma
in the following way:

•

Constraints on
\sigma
imply that no edge of
{\sf BoundaryEdges}
is crossed by any other edge.

•

For any pair of edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\sf RegularEdges}
, if they cross according to
\sigma
(in the usual sense), then we count it as a single crossing.

•

If an edge
e_{r}\in{\sf RegularEdges}
and an edge
e_{m}\in{\sf WeightedEdges}
with multiplicity
\mu(e_{m})
cross in
\sigma
(in the usual sense), then we count it as
\mu(e_{m})
crossings.

•

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\sf RegularEdges}
be two multi-edges.

–

If
e_{1},e_{2}
are incident to different endpoints of the same edge
e\in{\sf BoundaryEdges}
, then they will necessarily cross in any drawing
\sigma
, that satisfies the constraints above. However we count zero crossings in
\sigma
.
13
13

13

These crossings will be already accounted for while creating the extended instance.

–

Otherwise, if
e_{1},e_{2}
cross in
\sigma
(in the usual sense), then we count as
\mu(e_{1})\cdot\mu(e_{2})
crossings.

From the original instance to extended instance.

Given the original instance
\mathcal{I}^{o}=(H,U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},k^{\star})
of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
. First, we try each value of
0\leq k\leq k^{\star}
(these are
k+1
guesses). Next, we also guess the partition
k=k_{12}+k_{23}
, such that if
\mathcal{I}
is a
Yes
-instance, then it admits a drawing
\sigma
with
k
crossings, then there are
k_{12}
crossings among the edges of
E_{12}
and
k_{23}
crossings among the edges of
E_{23}
. There are
k\leq k^{\star}
guesses for this. Next, we guess the leftmost and rightmost vertices in
U_{1}
and
U_{3}
in
\sigma
, for which there are at most
4
choices, and hence the number of guesses is at most
n^{4}
. Fix one such guess, and let us call the leftmost (resp. rightmost) vertices in
U_{1}
and
U_{3}
as
u^{l}_{1},u^{l}_{3}
(resp.
u^{r}_{1},u^{r}_{3}
), respectively. Now, we add two new vertices
u^{l}_{2},u^{r}_{2}
, and add edges
(u^{l}_{1},u^{l}_{2}),(u^{l}_{2},u^{l}_{3}),(u^{r}_{1},u^{r}_{2}),(u^{r}_{2},u^{r}_{3})
, respectively, and denote them as
{\sf leftbound}_{12},{\sf leftbound}_{23},{\sf rightbound}_{12},{\sf rightbound}_{23}
, respectively. By slightly abusing the notation, we continue to use
U_{i}
for the new sets of vertices. Note that we have added
2
additional vertices and
4
additional edges, which only increases the size of the instance by
\mathcal{O}(1)
. Now, it can be easily observed that, given a drawing for the original instance
\mathcal{I}^{o}
that satisfies
\pi_{i}(u^{l}_{i})\leq\pi_{i}(u)\leq\pi_{i}(u^{r}_{i})
for
i\in\left\{1,3\right\}
(if the guess is correct, then
\sigma
is such a drawing), one can obtain a new drawing of the new instance by placing
u^{l}_{2}
at the extreme left on
V_{2}
and
u^{r}_{2}
on the extreme right on
V_{2}
. Further, this does not create any additional crossings. Thus, the following observation is immediate.

Observation 6
.

\mathcal{I}^{o}
is a
Yes
-instance iff at least one of the at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
extended instances
\mathcal{I}
obtained after this guessing is an extended
Yes
-instance.

Henceforth, we may drop the qualifier
extended
from
extended instance
, and simply say
instance
/
Yes
-instance
, respectively. Further, the graph
G
in an instance

\mathcal{I}=(G,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},\pi,k,k_{12},k_{23})
is said to be the
underlying graph
of the instance
\mathcal{I}
, and
k
is said to be its parameter.

Next, we continue to adopt the notation of
{\sf cross}(\cdot)
and that of red and blue edges from
Definition

4
and
Definition

5
. Further, note that
3
continues to hold.

5.2
Algorithm

We use
n
and
m
to denote the number of vertices and edges, respectively.
14
14

14

Note that if we start with the kernelized instance, then
n
and
m
are polynomial in the original parameter
k^{\star}
; however we will only use the kernelization to derive our final theorem.
Next, we transform it into an extended instance, as described above. This increases the number of vertices and edges by at most
4
each, and from
6
it follows that the kernelized instance is a
Yes
-instance iff for at least one of the guesses, we have an
Yes
-instance. For each extended instance corresponding to each of the guesses, we will run the following algorithm.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(G,V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},\pi,k,k_{12},k_{23})
be the extended instance given as an input. Let
c
be a sufficiently large constant. In two special cases, our algorithm directly solves the given instance by a careful enumeration. These two cases correspond to, when (i)
k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, or (ii)
\min\left\{|E_{12}|,|E_{23}|\right\}\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, respectively. We first explain these two base cases. Otherwise, when
\min\left\{|E_{12}|,|E_{23}|\right\}>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, and
k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, we are in the recursive case, explained later.

Base case.

We have the following sub-cases.

Case 1.
k<c\sqrt{(k^{\star})}
.

This base case is handled exactly as in
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
via
Proposition

2
(
[
3
]
), which also holds for three layers.

Case 2.
\min\left\{|E_{12}|,|E_{23}|\right\}<c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
.

15
15

15

This base case very closely resembles the way we solve the “middle” subproblem in the recursive case. In fact, we could create an extended instance corresponding to the middle subproblem and make a recursive call, which will immediately trigger this base case. However, we explicitly give the reduction to the two-layer case in the middle subproblem for the ease of presentation.

Note here that we count the edges without multiplicity similar to the analogous base case for
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
. Now, suppose w.l.o.g. that
|E_{23}|<c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
(the other case is exactly analogous). Let
V^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq V_{2}
be the set of all the vertices defined as follows:

V^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:u\text{ has a neighbor in }V_{3}\right\}\cup{\sf ExtBnd}.

We know that
|V_{3}|\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
(note that each vertex of
V_{3}
must have at least one edge incident to it), and
|V^{\prime}_{2}|\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}+\alpha\sqrt{k^{\star}}
. We guess permutations
\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3}
of
V^{\prime}_{2},V_{3}
for which there are
(k^{\star})^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
choices. We terminate the guess for any choice that violates the conditions of a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
, given in
Definition

15
, specifically, this includes the case when the number of crossings among the edges of
E_{23}
according to
\zeta
is not equal to
k_{23}
. Otherwise, for each non-terminated guess, we create an instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},W_{1},W_{2},{\sf ExtBnd}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},k^{\prime})
of
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
as follows.

•

W_{1}=V_{1}
and
W_{2}\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:u\text{ has a neighbor in }V_{1}\right\}\cup{\sf ExtBnd}
.

•

{\sf ExtBnd}^{\prime}={\sf ExtBnd}={\sf LeftExtBnd}^{\prime}\cup{\sf RightExtBnd}^{\prime}
, where
{\sf LeftExtBnd}^{\prime}={\sf LeftExtBnd},{\sf RightExtBnd}^{\prime}={\sf RightExtBnd}
.

•

{\sf BoundaryEdges}^{\prime}=\left\{{\sf leftbound}^{\prime}\cup{\sf rightbound}^{\prime}\right\}
, where
{\sf leftbound}^{\prime}={\sf leftbound}_{23},{\sf rightbound}^{\prime}={\sf rightbound}_{23}
.

•

k^{\prime}=k_{12}

•

\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{2})
, where
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{1}
, and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}\cup\zeta_{2}
.

It is straightforward to check that the instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a valid elaborate extended instance of
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
as per
Definition

2
. Then, we solve this instance using
Theorem

6
(without kernelization) and find a drawing if it is a
Yes
-instance. Each such call takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
, and there are at most
(k^{\star})^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
recursive calls. Thus, the overall time taken in this base case is at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
.

If for some instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, the algorithm returns a drawing
\varsigma^{\prime}=(\varsigma^{\prime}_{1},\varsigma^{\prime}_{2})
of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, then we can obtain a combined drawing
\sigma^{\prime}=(\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}_{3})
of
\mathcal{I}
as follows:
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}=\varsigma^{\prime}_{1}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}=\zeta_{2}\cup\varsigma^{\prime}_{2}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{3}=\zeta_{3}
. It is straightforward to check that
\sigma^{\prime}
is a valid drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Otherwise, if all guesses are terminated, or the two-layer algorithm returns
No
, then we conclude that
\mathcal{I}
is a
No
-instance. This finishes the description of the second base case.

Lemma 10
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an extended instance of
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
with underlying graph
G
and parameter
k
. If either (i)
k\leq c\sqrt{(k^{\star})}
, or (ii)
\min\left\{|E_{12}|,|E_{23}|\right\}\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
. Then, the base case of the algorithm as described above returns a drawing
\sigma
of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}
iff
\mathcal{I}
is an extended
Yes
-instance.

Otherwise, we proceed to the recursive case, explained next.

Recursive case.

Since the base case is not applicable, we know that
k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
, and
\min\left\{|E_{12}|,|E_{23}|\right\}>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
for some sufficiently large constant
c
. In this case, at a high level, our recursive algorithm consists of the following steps.

1.

Divide.
First, we prove some combinatorial properties of a “nice separator” in this step, which is defined by a set of at most
4
edges, and
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
additional relevant vertices and edges. This separator divides the instance into at most three instances having certain properties. We refer to these sub-instances as
left, middle
, and
right
sub-instances. Note that, of these, the left and right sub-instances will also be extended instances.

2.

Guess the interfaces.
In this step, we actually guess various components of the separator (whose existence is guaranteed in the previous step, assuming that we are dealing with a
Yes
-instance), other relevant information about the separator, and the rest of the interface between the sub-instances, i.e., the vertices and edges that span across multiple sub-instances.

Due to the properties of the separator, this will constitute
n^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2/3})}
guesses.

3.

Creating sub-instances.
Formally defining the sub-instances based on the choices guessed so far.

The construction of each sub-instance takes
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.

4.

Conquer left and right.
Recursively solving the left and right instances.

Inductively, this takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}((3k/4)^{2/3})}
.

5.

Conquer middle.
If, for a particular guess, both sub-instances are found to be
Yes
-instances, then we will use the solutions for left and right sub-problems, plus some
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
guesses to reduce the problem to
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
, which can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
using the algorithm from the previous section (
Theorem

6
).

6.

Obtaining a combined drawing.
Assuming that for some guess, both the recursive calls corresponding to left and right instances output the corresponding drawings, and we find a “compatible” drawing for the middle subproblem via
Theorem

6
, then we combine the drawings along with the guesses made for the middle subproblem, in order to obtain a combined drawing for the current sub-instance. This step takes polynomial time for each guess.

Let
T_{3}(n,k)
denote an upper bound on the running time of our algorithm on an extended instance with a graph with at most
n
vertices and with parameter bounded by
k
. From the above description, it follows that
T_{3}(n,k)
satisfies the following recurrence.

\displaystyle T_{3}(n,k)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}n^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2/3})}\cdot(T_{3}(n,\frac{3k}{4})+T_{2}(n,k))+n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}&\text{ if }k>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\text{ and }|E|>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\\
n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k^{\star}})}&\text{ if }k\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\text{ or }|E|\leq c\sqrt{k^{\star}}\end{cases}

(2)

Here,
T_{2}(n,k)=n^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
is the running time of the algorithm for
2
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
when we directly use the algorithm of
Theorem

6
without the kernelization step. Then, it can be shown that this recurrence solves to
T_{3}(n,k^{\star})=n^{\mathcal{O}((k^{\star})^{2/3})}
.

5.3
Step 1: Divide.

This step is subdivided into two steps. In
Section

5.3.1
, we will show the existence of a “balanced separator” and explore its various properties. Then, in
Section

5.3.2
, we will show a technical claim that lets us obtain a more structured drawing (for a
Yes
-instance) that is still compatible with the separator from the previous step. Now, we explore each of these steps in more detail.

5.3.1
Existence and Properties of a Separator

Definition 16

(Bottom- and Top-heavy instances)
.

If, in the given instance we have that
k_{12}\geq k_{23}
, i.e.,
k_{12}\geq\frac{k}{2}
, then say that such an instance is
bottom-heavy
; and otherwise if
k_{23}<k_{12}
, then we say that an instance is
top-heavy
.

For the rest of this section while describing the algorithm, we will assume that we are dealing with a bottom-heavy instance, without repeating this assumption at each step. Specifically, this assumption is crucial while defining the structure of the separator defined in this subsection, which follows. Otherwise, the top-heavy instances can be dealt with interchanging the roles of the first and third lines in the definition of separator (
Definition

17
) and the rest of the algorithm.

We adopt and naturally extend the notions of edge orderings from
Definition

6
as well as that of an ordering between an edge and a crossing from
Definition

7
to
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
. We note that an analogous version of
4
holds in
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
as well.

Definition 17
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal bottom-heavy
Yes
-instance, and let
\sigma
be any drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. We say that
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12},{\sf lsep}_{23},{\sf rsep}_{23}\right\}
is a
separator
w.r.t.
\sigma
, if the following properties hold (see
Figure

18
for an example of
{\sf Sep}
and associated objects.).

1.

{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12}\in E_{12}
are two distinct blue edges w.r.t.
\sigma
and they do not cross each other

{\sf lsep}_{23},{\sf rsep}_{23}\in E_{23}
are two distinct blue edges w.r.t.
\sigma
and they do not cross each other.

2.

\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12}),1)\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12}),1)
,

\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23}),2)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2))\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))
,

\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23}),3)\leq\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23}),3)
.

3.

The number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
, and that to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
is at most
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
.

4.

Let
{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)
denote the set of all edges
u_{1}u_{2}\in E_{12}
other than
{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12}
, such that

\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12}),i)\leq\sigma_{i}(u_{i})\leq\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12}),i)\text{ for }i\in[2].

Then
|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
.

5.

Let
V^{conn}_{2}\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:N(u)\cap V_{3}\neq\emptyset\right\}
, and define

\displaystyle{\sf GapLeft}({\sf Sep},\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{v\in V^{conn}_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23}),2)<\sigma_{2}(v)<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12}),2)\right\}

\displaystyle{\sf GapRight}({\sf Sep},\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{v\in V^{conn}_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12}),2)<\sigma_{2}(v)<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23}),2)\right\}

Then,
|{\sf GapLeft}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|,|{\sf GapRight}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|\leq 3\sqrt{k}
.

6.

|{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)|\leq 4\sqrt{k}
, where
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)\coloneqq\bigcup_{e\in{\sf Sep}}{\sf cross}(e,\sigma)
.

When the separator
{\sf Sep}
and/or the drawing
\sigma
are obvious from the context, we may simply use
{\sf GapLeft},{\sf GapRight},

{\sf MiddleEdges}
, and
{\sf CrossingEdges}
.

We state an immediate consequence of the preceding definition.

Observation 7
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal bottom heavy extended
Yes
-instance, let
\sigma
be a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings, and let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
. Let
k^{l}_{12},k^{r}_{12}
denote the number of crossings that are to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
and to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
, respectively, and
k^{l}_{23},k^{r}_{23}
denote the number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
and to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{23}
, respectively. Then,
k^{l},k^{r}\leq 3k/4,
where
k^{l}\coloneqq k^{l}_{12}+k^{l}_{23}
and
k^{r}\coloneqq k^{r}_{12}+k^{r}_{23}
.

Proof.

Let
k_{12}=\alpha k
and
k_{23}=(1-\alpha)k
for some
\alpha\geq 1/2
, since
k_{12}\geq k/2\geq k_{23}
. Then, since
{\sf Sep}
is a separator,
k^{l}_{12}\leq k_{12}/2=\alpha k/2
, and
k^{r}_{12}\leq k_{23}=(1-\alpha)k
. Therefore,
k^{l}_{23}+k^{r}_{23}\leq k(\alpha/2+1-\alpha)=(1-\alpha/2)k\leq 3k/4
, where the last inequality follows from
\alpha\geq 1/2
. Analogous argument works for
k^{r}
.
∎

Figure 18:
Illustration of a separator from
Definition

17
.
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12},{\sf lsep}_{23},{\sf rsep}_{23}\right\}
are shown in
blue
, and
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
are shown in
purple
. Edges of
M\coloneqq{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
shown in
brown
and that of
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})
are shown in dashed
olive
– these are the edges that cross at least one edge of
{\sf Sep}
. Vertices of
GapLeft
are shown in
orange
and that of
GapRight
in
green
.

Next, we state the following important lemma that shows the existence of a separator, which forms the basis of our divide-and-conquer algorithm.

Lemma 11

(Separator Lemma for
3
-
Layer Crossing Minimization
)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a bottom-heavy
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance and let
\sigma
be any drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Then, there exists a separator
{\sf Sep}
w.r.t.
\sigma
.

Proof.

Note that due to
k
-minimality and bottom-heaviness of
\mathcal{I}
, we know that the number of crossings among the edges of
E_{12}
is larger than
k_{12}/2
; note here that
k_{12}/2<k_{12}
, since
k_{12}\geq k/2>c/2
for some absolute constant
c
. We divide the proof into four parts: (I ) constructing
{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12}
, (II) bounding the size of
{\sf MiddleEdges}
, (III) constructing
{\sf rsep}_{23}
and analyzing
{\sf GapRight}
, and (IV) constructing
{\sf lsep}_{23}
and analyzing
{\sf GapLeft}
. The first two parts are exactly identical to the proof of
Lemma

2
; nevertheless we include them here for the sake of completeness.

Part (I): Constructing
{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12}
.

We define a lexicographic order
\prec
on the edges of
E
using the order of their endpoints in
V_{1}
using
\sigma_{1}
, with ties broken using the endpoints in
V_{2}
using
\sigma_{2}
. More formally, for two distinct edges
e_{a},e_{b}\in E
, we have
e_{a}\prec e_{b}
iff
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},1))<\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},1))
, or
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},1))=\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},1))
and
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(e_{a},2))<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(e_{b},2))
(multi-edges are treated as a single edge for this order). Let
{\sf rsep}_{12}\in E(G)
be the first blue edge, according to
\prec
, that has more than
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
crossings to its left. First we claim that
{\sf rsep}_{12}
exists, since
{\sf rightbound}_{12}
—which is blue, and has all (
k_{12}>\frac{k_{12}}{2}
) crossings to its left— satisfies the stated property.

By the definition of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
, the number of crossings to its left is larger than
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
, which implies the following: (a) the number of crossings to its right is at most
\frac{k_{12}}{2}\leq\frac{k}{2}
(note that some crossings may not be either to the left or to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
). (b) the number of edges to its left is at least
\frac{k_{12}}{2}>0
. See
Figure

18
for an illustration. Further, (b) implies that
{\sf leftbound}_{12}
is one of the
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
edges that is to the left of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
, which specifically implies that
{\sf leftbound}_{12}\neq{\sf rsep}_{12}
.

Now, we define
{\sf lsep}_{12}
to be the last edge according to
\prec
such that: (i)
{\sf lsep}_{12}
is blue, (ii)
{\sf lsep}_{12}\prec{\sf rsep}_{12}
, and (iii)
{\sf lsep}_{12}
does not cross
{\sf rsep}_{12}
according to
\sigma
. First,
{\sf lsep}_{12}
exists because
{\sf leftbound}_{12}
satisfies conditions (i) - (iii) – indeed
{\sf leftbound}_{12}\neq{\sf rsep}_{12}
as shown above. Further, since
{\sf lsep}_{12}\prec{\sf rsep}_{12}
,
{\sf lsep}_{12}
is to the left of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
and in particular the two edges do not cross each other. Now, if the number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
is larger than
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
, then this contradicts the choice of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
as the lexicographically first blue edge with more than
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
crossings to its left. Therefore, the number of crossings to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
is at most
\frac{k_{12}}{2}
. This finishes the first part.

Part (II): Bounding the size of
{\sf MiddleEdges}
.

Suppose for the contradiction
|{\sf MiddleEdges}|>2\sqrt{k}
. Note that the definition of
{\sf MiddleEdges}
, for each edge
e\in{\sf MiddleEdges}
,
{\sf lsep}_{12}\prec e\prec{\sf rsep}_{12}
, and
e
does not cross
{\sf rsep}_{12}
. Then, by
3
,
{\sf MiddleEdges}
contains at least one blue edge, say
b
. However, note that
b
satisfies conditions (i)–(iii) above, which contradicts the choice of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
, since
{\sf lsep}_{12}
is the lexicographically last such edge. This shows that
|{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
.

Part (III): Constructing
{\sf rsep}_{23}
and analyzing
{\sf GapRight}
.

Now we define a similar lexicographic order
\prec^{\prime}
on the edges of
E_{23}
, except first we consider the order of the
V_{2}
endpoints, and then the ties are broken using
V_{3}
endpoints (multi-edges are treated as a single edge for this order).

Let
{\sf rsep}_{23}
be a lexicographically first blue edge according to
\prec^{\prime}
such that (i)
{\sf rsep}_{23}\neq{\sf leftbound}_{23}
, and (ii)
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12}),2)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23}),2)
, if it exists.
16
16

16

Note that, if
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf rightbound}_{23},2))
, then
{\sf rightbound}_{23}
satisfies both the conditions (indeed
{\sf rightbound}_{23}\neq{\sf leftbound}_{23}
since
|E_{23}|>c\sqrt{k^{\star}}
). In case
{\sf rsep}_{23}
does not exist, then we define
{\sf rsep}_{23}
to be a “phantom (placeholder) edge”, and
{\sf GapLeft}=\emptyset
. When such an edge will be chosen for dividing the problem into sub-problems, the right sub-problem will be a degenerate instance with zero edges in
E_{23}
, which can be then solved by the second base case. We omit the details.

Then, consider the set of vertices
{\sf GapRight}
as defined above. First, if
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2)
, then
{\sf GapRight}=\emptyset
. Now, consider
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2)
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that
|{\sf GapRight}|>2\sqrt{k}
. Let
E^{r}=\left\{wz\in E_{23}:w\in{\sf GapRight},z\in V_{3}\right\}
. Note that
|E^{r}|\geq|{\sf GapRight}|>2\sqrt{k}
, since each
w\in{\sf GapRight}
contributes at least one edge to
E^{r}
, and these edges are distinct. Then, by
3
,
E^{r}
contains at least one blue edge
b
. Note that
b\prec^{\prime}{\sf rsep}_{23}
, since
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(b,2))<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2))
by construction, which contradicts the choice of
{\sf rsep}_{23}
as the lexicographically first such edge. Thus,
|{\sf GapRight}|\leq 2\sqrt{k}\leq 3\sqrt{k}
.

Part (IV). Constructing
{\sf lsep}_{23}
and analyzing
{\sf GapLeft}
.

We define
{\sf lsep}_{23}
to be the lexicographically last blue edge (if it exists) such that (i)
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23}),2)\geq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12}),2)
, and (ii)
{\sf lsep}_{23}
does not cross
{\sf rsep}_{23}
.
17
17

17

A degenerate corner case arises when
{\sf leftbound}_{23}
does not satisfy the conditions, and hence
{\sf lsep}_{23}
does not exist. This can be handled analogous to the previous case. We omit the details.

Now consider
{\sf GapLeft}
as defined above. First, if
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2)
, then
{\sf GapLeft}=\emptyset
. Now, suppose
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2)
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that,
|{\sf GapLeft}|>2\sqrt{k}
. Then, let
E^{l}=\left\{wz\in E_{23}:w\in{\sf GapLeft},z\in V_{3}\right\}
. Note that by a similar argument,
|E^{l}|\geq|V^{l}|>3\sqrt{k}
, since each
w\in V^{l}
contributes at least one edge to
E^{r}
. Let
E^{\prime r}\subseteq E^{r}
be the set of edges that do not cross
{\sf rsep}_{23}
. Note that
|E^{\prime r}|\geq|E^{r}|-\sqrt{k}>2\sqrt{k}
, since
{\sf rsep}_{23}
is a blue edge. Then, by
3
,
E^{\prime r}
contains at least one blue edge, say
b^{\prime}
. Further,
{\sf lsep}_{23}\prec^{\prime}b
since
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2))<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}(b^{\prime},2))
. This contradicts the choice of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
as the lexicographically last edge. This shows that
|{\sf GapLeft}|\leq 3\sqrt{k}
.

∎

Note that a symmetric version of
Definition

17
and
Lemma

11
holds for top-heavy instances. We omit the definition and the proof.

In the following definition, we divide (not necessarily in disjoint sets) the vertex sets into three parts based on a drawing of an extended instance. These definitions will be used later on during the analysis of the algorithm.

Definition 18
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
Yes
-instance with underlying graph
G
, and let
\sigma
be a drawing of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Consider a separator
{\sf Sep}
w.r.t.
\sigma
along with the associated objects as in
Definition

17
. Then, we define the following division of vertices on each layer into left (L), middle (M), and right (R) parts, as follows.

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}(u)\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)\leq\sigma_{1}(u)\leq\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{1}:\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1))\leq\sigma_{1}(u)\right\}

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)\right\}\cup{\sf GapLeft}\cup{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2)

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V^{conn}_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2))\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\left\{u\in V^{conn}_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2))\leq\sigma_{2}(u)\right\}\cup{\sf GapRight}\cup{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2)

\displaystyle V_{3}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{3}:\sigma_{3}(u)\leq\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{3}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal M})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{3}:\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},1)\leq\sigma_{3}(u)\leq\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},1))\right\}

\displaystyle V_{3}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal R})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{3}:\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))\leq\sigma_{3}(u)\right\}

Finally, for each
{\cal T}\in\left\{{\cal L},{\cal M},{\cal R}\right\}
, define
V(\sigma,{\cal T})\coloneqq V_{1}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})\cup V_{2}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})\cup V_{3}(\sigma,{\sf Sep},{\cal T})
.

Now, fix a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, a corresponding drawing
\sigma
of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings, and a separator
{\sf Sep}
(and the associated objects from
Definition

17
) as guaranteed by
Lemma

11
. In the next part, we classify the components in
G-v
for
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
into multiple types, and then analyze their behavior in
\sigma
. This is largely similar to the two layer case; except that the analogue of pendant components for the three layer case is technically much more challenging to analyze, which is the focus of the next subsection.

Definition 19
.

Let
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
be a vertex, and let
{\sf comps}(v)
denote the set of connected components of
C_{v}-v
, where
C_{v}
is the component of
G
that contains
v
. We partition these components into different classes (subsets), called
{\sf Special}(v),\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v),{\sf UpDown}(v),
and
{\sf Peculiar}(v)
(we may omit the subscript
G
). A component
C\in{\sf comps}_{G}(v)
belongs to:

•

{\sf Special}(v)
, if
E(G[C\cup\left\{v\right\}])
contains an edge of
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep})\cup{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
, or if
C
contains a vertex of
{\sf GapLeft}\cup{\sf GapRight}\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\setminus\left\{v\right\}
.

•

\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, if
C\not\in{\sf Special}(v)
, and
C
contains a vertex
u\in\bigcup_{i\in[3]}\bigcup_{j\in[\lambda]}V^{j}_{i}

18
18

18

Recall that
V^{j}_{i}
’s are the
\lambda
subsets over which the total orders
\pi^{j}_{i}
are defined, and these
\lambda
total orders constitute
\pi_{i}
.

•

{\sf UpDown}(v)
, if it does not belong to
{\sf Special}(v)\cup\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, and
C
contains at least one vertex on the same line as
v
.

•

{\sf Peculiar}(v)
, if it does not belong to
{\sf Special}(v)\cup\pi\text{\sf-Defined}(v)
, and
C
does not contain a vertex on the same layer as
v
.

We also define a subset
{\sf Pendants}(v)\subseteq{\sf Peculiar}(v)
consisting of components that consist of a single pendant vertex adjacent to
v
(possibly with a multi-edge).

Note that if
v\in V_{2}
, then
{\sf Pendants}(v)={\sf Peculiar}(v)
. However, if
v\in V_{1}\cup V_{3}
, then we define
{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)\coloneqq{\sf Peculiar}(v)\setminus{\sf Pendants}(v)
(peculiar non-pendants), and these are the components that contain vertices of the remaining two layers other than that of
v
.

First, the following observation follows from the bounds on
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep}),{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
,
{\sf GapLeft}
, and
{\sf GapRight}
.

Observation 8
.

For any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, it holds that
|{\sf Special}(v)|\leq 12\sqrt{k}+8=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
.

Next, we bound the size of
{\sf UpDown}
in the following lemma, which is done via arguments almost identical to that in
Lemma

3
.

Lemma 12
.

For any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
, it holds that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 8\sqrt{k}
.

Proof.

Consider any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\cap(V_{1}\cap V_{3})
. Then, one can use arguments exactly as in
Lemma

3
to show that, in fact,
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 4\sqrt{k}\leq 8\sqrt{k}
. Now, consider
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\cap V_{2}={\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},2)
, and suppose that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|>8\sqrt{k}
. Then, we classify the components of
{\sf UpDown}(v)
into two types, say (12) and (32), where type 12 components contain a neighbor of
v
in
V_{1}
, and its neighbor in
V_{2}
(it may possibly contain vertices of
V_{3}
); whereas type 32 components contain a neighbor of
v
in
V_{3}
and its neighbor in
V_{2}
. If a component satisfies both conditions, then arbitrarily place it in one of them. At least the number of components of either of the two types of components must be larger than
4\sqrt{k}
. Then, one can use an argument similar to that in
Lemma

3
to arrive at a contradiction. This proves that for any
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},2)
, it holds that
|{\sf UpDown}(v)|\leq 8\sqrt{k}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

To analyze the peculiar components, we again need to consider different cases based on the structure of the separator.

Definition 20
.

Let
{\sf Sep}=\left\{{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{12},{\sf lsep}_{23},{\sf rsep}_{23}\right\}
be the separator as guaranteed by
Lemma

11
. We have the following different (non-exclusive) cases.

(i)
Distinct Bottom (DB)
:

if
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
.

Shared Bottom (SB):
if
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
.

(ii)
Distinct Top (DT)
:

if
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)\neq{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
.

Shared Top (ST):
if
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
.

Based on this, we have the following disjoint cases: (
DB
,
DT
), (
DB
,
ST
), (
SB
,
DT
), (
SB
,
ST
).

Next, we introduce an additional definition that further classifies the components of .

Definition 21
.

We say that a vertex subset
C\in V_{1}\cup V_{2}
(resp.
C\in V_{2}\cup V_{3})
is to the left of
e\in E_{12}
(resp.
E_{23}
), if for each
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
(resp.
i\in\left\{2,3\right\}
) it holds that for all
u\in C\cap V_{i}
,
\sigma_{i}(u)\leq\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}(e,i))
. Analogously, we define “right of” relation if the inequality is flipped.

•

For each
v\in\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1),{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)\right\}
, let
{\sf Peculiar}^{l}(v)\subseteq{\sf Peculiar}(v)

(resp.
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v)\subseteq{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
) be the set of components
C
that are to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
.

•

For each
v\in\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1),{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)\right\}
, let
{\sf Peculiar}^{r}(v)\subseteq{\sf Peculiar}(v)

(resp.
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}(v)\subseteq{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
) be the set of components
C
that are to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{23}
.

•

For each
v\in\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},1),{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},1)\right\}
, let
{\sf Peculiar}^{l}(v)\subseteq{\sf Peculiar}(v)

(resp.
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v)\subseteq{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
) be the set of components
C
that are to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
.

•

For each
v\in\left\{{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},1),{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},1)\right\}
, let
{\sf Peculiar}^{r}(v)\subseteq{\sf Peculiar}(v)

(resp.
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}(v)\subseteq{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
) be the set of components
C
that are to the left of
{\sf rsep}_{23}
.

We next prove the following lemma.

Lemma 13
.

The following bounds hold:

1.

\min\left\{|{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)),{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))|\right\}\leq\sqrt{k}
.

2.

\min\left\{|{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)),{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))|\right\}\leq\sqrt{k}
.

Proof.

We will show the first item, and the proof of the second one is analogous. Let us simplify the notation and say
x_{1}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1),x_{2}\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)
.
Suppose for the contradiction that
|{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})|>\sqrt{k}
and
|{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})|>\sqrt{k}
. Note that
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})\cap{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{2})=\emptyset
, since a component
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})
contains a neighbor of
x_{1}
and hence in
G-{\sf endpt}(x_{2})
,
V(C)\cup\left\{x_{2}\right\}
is part of one connected component whose vertex set is a strict superset of that of
C
. In the next paragraph, we will show that each pair of components
C_{1}\in{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})
and
C_{2}\in{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{2})
must account for a distinct crossing. This implies that the number of crossings between the components of
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{1})
and
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(x_{2})
is larger than
k
, a contradiction.

Let
u_{1}\in V(C_{1})\cap V_{2}
be the rightmost neighbor of
x_{1}
in
C
, and let
w_{1}\in V(C_{1})\cap V_{3}
be the rightmost vertex in
V(C_{1})\cap V_{3}
. Similarly, define
u_{2}\in V(C_{2})\cap V_{2}
as the rightmost neighbor of
x_{2}
in
C_{2}
and let
w_{2}\in V_{1}\cap V(C_{2})
be the rightmost vertex of
V(C_{2})\cap V_{1}
. If
w_{2}
has a neighbor
q_{2}
that is to the right of
u_{1}
, then
w_{2}q_{2}
crosses
x_{1}u_{1}
(see
Figure

19
, case (a)). Otherwise, if all neighbors of
w_{2}
are to the left of
u_{1}
. Let
q_{2}
be one such neighbor of
w_{2}
. There exists a path between
u_{2}
and
q_{2}
that is entirely within
C_{1}
. Since
u_{1}
is between
q_{2}
and
u_{2}
, such a path must cross either the edge
x_{1}u_{1}
or the edge
u_{1}w_{1}
(see
Figure

19
, case (b)).

Figure 19:
Illustration for the proof of
Lemma

13
. In case (b), we show two possible paths from
q_{2}
to
u_{2}
– the green path crosses
x_{1}u_{1}
; whereas the red path crosses
u_{1}w_{1}
.

∎

In the next observation, we consolidate some properties regarding the components
{\sf comps}(v)
for
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
in different cases.

Lemma 14
.

1.

In
DB
case,

(a)

{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1))
. Further, each
C\in{\sf Pendants}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)
lies to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)
.

(b)

{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)={\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1))
. Further, each
C\in{\sf Pendants}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
lies to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
.

2.

In
DT
case,

(a)

{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)={\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))
.

(b)

{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},1)={\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))
.

3.

In
SB
case, when
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)=v_{b}
, say, then
({\sf Peculiar}^{l}(v_{b}),{\sf Peculiar}^{r}(v_{b}))
is a partition of
{\sf Peculiar}(v_{b})
.

4.

In
ST
case, when
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23})=v_{t}
, say, then
({\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v_{t}),{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}(v_{t}))
is a partition of
{\sf PeculiarNP}(v_{t})
.

Proof.

1.

DB case.
We give the proof of item 1 and the proof of 2 is analogous. Let
v={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)
, and we will prove that
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v)={\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
. Consider a component
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
, and suppose for the contradiction that there exists some edge
e=uw\in E(C)
that is not to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
. If
e={\sf lsep}_{23}
, or if
e
crosses
{\sf lsep}_{23}
, then
C
would belong to
{\sf Special}(v)
, which is a contradiction.
Now, suppose that
e=uw
is to the right of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
, where
u\in V_{2}
and
w\in V_{3}
. Since
C
is a connected component in
G-v
, there is a path
P=(v=v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}=u)
such that
vv_{1}\in E(G)
, and
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(C)
for
1\leq i\leq\ell-1
. Let us also observe that, except for
v\in V_{1}
, all other vertices in
P
alternate between vertices of
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
, with odd indexed vertices belonging to
V_{2}
and even indexed vertices belonging to
V_{3}
, – if
P
contained a vertex of
V_{1}
, then
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
, a contradiction.

If
\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))<\sigma_{3}(w)
, then we say that this is
case 1
, otherwise if
\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))<\sigma_{(}w)<\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))
, then we say that this is
case 2
.

In
case 1
, note that there exists an edge of the path
P
must either cross
{\sf lsep}_{23}
,
{\sf rsep}_{23}
, or pass through a vertex of
{\sf GapRight}
. In each of the cases, this implies that
C\in{\sf Special}(v)
, which is a contradiction.

Now, let us consider
case 2
. Let
v_{j}\in C\cap V_{2}
,
j>0
be the first vertex along the path that lies to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)
. Note that
v_{j}
exists, since the last vertex, i.e.,
u
satisfies the condition. We will show that in this case,
C
must belong to
{\sf Special}(v)
, a contradiction.

First, if
j=1
, then let us consider two cases. (i) If
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2))<\sigma_{2}(v_{1})<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2))
, then
v_{1}\in{\sf GapLeft}
. (ii) If
\sigma_{2}(v_{1})<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2))<\sigma_{2}(v_{1})<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))
, then
vv_{1}\in{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
. (iii) Finally, if
\sigma_{2}(v_{1})>\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},2))
, then
vv_{1}
crosses
{\sf rsep}_{12}
. Thus, in each of the three sub-cases, we obtain that
C\in{\sf Special}(v)
, which is a contradiction. Note that this same argument also shows the second claim about components from
{\sf Pendants}(v)
.

Next, suppose
j>1
. First, note that
j\geq 3
, and
v_{j-1}\in V_{3}
. (i) If
\sigma_{3}(v_{j-1})<\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))
, then the edge
v_{j-1}v_{j}
crosses
{\sf lsep}_{12}
. (ii) Otherwise,
\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))<\sigma_{3}(v_{j-1})
. Note that
v_{j-2}\in V_{2}
satisfies that
\sigma_{2}(v_{j-2})<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2))
. However, this implies that the edge
v_{j-2}v_{j-1}
crosses
{\sf lsep}_{23}
. Thus, again in each of the two sub-cases, we obtain that
C\in{\sf Special}(v)
, a contradiction.

2.

DT case.
Again, we prove the first item, and the proof of the second item is analogous. Again, for convenience, let
v={\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)
, and consider any
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
. Suppose for contradiction that
C\not\in{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v)
, which implies that
C
contains an edge
uw
with
u\in V_{2},w\in V_{1}
that is not to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12}
. Let us again consider a path
P=(v=v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\ell}=u)
where
vv_{1}\in E(G)
and
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(C)
. Again, the vertices must alternate between
V_{2}
and
V_{1}
respectively. Let
v_{j}\in V_{2}
be the first vertex along the path that is to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},2)
.

If
j=1
, then consider the following cases. (i)
\sigma_{1}(v_{2})<\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1))
, then the edge
v_{1}v_{2}
crosses
{\sf lsep}_{12}
. (ii) If
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1))<\sigma_{1}(v_{2})<\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1))
, then
v_{1}v_{2}\in{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
. (iii) If
\sigma_{1}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1))<\sigma_{2}(v_{2})
, then
v_{1}v_{2}
crosses
{\sf rsep}_{12}
. Thus, in each case
C\in{\sf Special}(v)
, a contradiction.

If
j>1
, then this proof is analogous to a similar case from the previous part, and hence we omit it.

3.

SB case.
In this case, suppose for contradiction that there exists some
C\in{\sf Peculiar}(v_{b})
such that
C\not\in{\sf Peculiar}^{l}(v_{b})\cup{\sf Peculiar}^{r}(v_{b})
. We show that
C\in{\sf Special}(v_{b})
, a contradiction. First, if
C
is a pendant vertex
u
, then using an argument similar to
DB case
above, we can show that either
u\in{\sf GapLeft}\cup{\sf GapRight}
, or the edge
v_{b}u\in{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
, a contradiction.
Note that for some
i\in\left\{2,3\right\}
, there exists a vertex
w\in V(C)\cap V_{i}
such that
\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},i))<\sigma_{i}(w)<\sigma_{i}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},i))
. Suppose w.l.o.g. that
w\in V_{2}
. Then, let
w^{\prime}\in V_{3}
be a neighbor of
w
in
C
. Note that
w^{\prime}
must also satisfy
\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))<\sigma_{3}(w^{\prime})<\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))
– otherwise
ww^{\prime}
edge crosses either
{\sf lsep}_{23}
or
{\sf rsep}_{23}
, a contradiction. However, if
w^{\prime}
lies between the top endpoints of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
and
{\sf rsep}_{23}
, then we can show that
C\in{\sf Special}(v_{b})
using an argument similar to the
DB
case above, and arrive at a contradiction.

4.

ST case.
Here, the argument is exactly similar to the
SB case
, and is therefore omitted. Note that in this case, the argument regarding the pendant vertices does not hold, since the edges of
{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep})
are only defined in
E_{23}
.

∎

Handling Pendant Components.

In this step, we will handle the pendant components attached to
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
. At a high level, the idea is similar to that used for the 2 layer case, except that we may have at most 8 vertices in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
.
19
19

19

By a more careful case analysis, it may be shown that we do not have to handle each of the 8 vertices, which further restricts the number of guesses; however since this does not substantially affect the running time, we handle all the vertices in
{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
in a unified fashion.

Definition 22
.

•

A
pendant-blueprint
is a collection of tuples

{\sf PendantBlueprint}=\left\{({\sf LeftPNum}(v),{\sf MidPNum}(v),{\sf RightPNum}(v)):v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\right\},

such that, (i) for each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
,
{\sf LeftPNum}(v)+{\sf MidPNum}(v)+{\sf RightPNum}(v)={\sf TotalPNum}(v)
, which denotes the total sum of multiplicities of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
, and (ii) for each
v^{\prime}\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)
,
{\sf MidPNum}(v)=0
.

•

A
pendant-guess
is a collection of tuples

{\sf PendantGuess}=\left\{({\sf LeftPendants}(v),{\sf MidPendants}(v),{\sf RightPendants}(v)):v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\right\}

where (i) for each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})
,
({\sf LeftPendants}(v),{\sf MidPendants}(v),{\sf RightPendants}(v))
is a partition of
{\sf Pendants}(v)
, and (ii) for each
v^{\prime}\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)
,
{\sf MidPendants}(v^{\prime})=\emptyset
.

•

We say that a
{\sf PendantGuess}=\left\{({\sf LeftPendants}(v),{\sf MidPNum}(v),{\sf RightPendants}(v)):v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})\right\}
is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant
if, for each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}(v)
the total sum of multiplicities of vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
(resp.
{\sf MidPNum}(v),{\sf RightPNum}(v)
) is equal to
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
(resp.
{\sf MidPendants}(v),{\sf RightPendants}(v)
).

•

Let
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
be a pendant-blueprint, and let
{\sf PendantGuess}
be a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant pendant-guess. Then, we define a
{\sf NicePFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess},{\sf Sep},\sigma)
to be a family of drawings
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying the following properties.

–

For each
i\in[3]
,
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
, when restricted to

\displaystyle V_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})}{\sf Pendants}(v)
, are the same.

–

For each
v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},1)\cup{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},3)
,

\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
places (i) all the vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)
, (ii) all the vertices of
{\sf MidPendants}(v)
between
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)
and
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)
, and (iii) all the vertices in
{\sf RightPendants}(v)
to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)
.

–

For each
v_{2}\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep},2)
,

*

\sigma^{\prime}_{1}
places (i) all the vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)
, (ii) all the vertices in
{\sf MidPendants}(v)
between
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2)
and
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2)
, and (iii) all the vertices in
{\sf RightPendants}(v)
to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
,

*

\sigma^{\prime}_{3}
places (i) all the vertices in
{\sf LeftPendants}(v)
to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)
, (ii) all the vertices in
{\sf MidPendants}(v)
between
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)
and
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3)
, and (iii) all the vertices in
{\sf RightPendants}(v)
to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3)
.

Next, we introduce the notion of separator-compatible drawings.

Definition 23
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be drawings with
k
crossings. Further, let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
.
We say that
\sigma^{\prime}
is a
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible drawing
if it satisfies the following properties.

•

{\sf Sep}
is also a separator w.r.t.
\sigma^{\prime}
, such that the number of crossings on each side of each edge in
{\sf Sep}
is same in
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
,

•

{\sf GapLeft}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf GapLeft}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
, and
{\sf GapRight}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf GapRight}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
.

•

{\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf MiddleEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
, and
{\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma)={\sf CrossingEdges}({\sf Sep},\sigma^{\prime})
.

Next, we state a transitive property of separator-compatibility.

Observation 9
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal extended instance, and let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
be drawings of
\mathcal{I}
with
k
crossings. Let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma_{1}
. If
\sigma_{2}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma_{1})
-compatible, and
\sigma_{3}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma_{2})
-compatible, then
\sigma_{3}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma_{1})
-compatible.

The proof of the following lemma is also via the same kinds of arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

5
, and thus is omitted.

Lemma 15
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal
Yes
-instance, let
\sigma
be a drawing with
k
crossings, and let
{\sf Sep}
be a separator guaranteed by
Lemma

2
.

Then, there exists a
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
such that, for any
{\sf PendantGuess}
that is
{\sf PendantBlueprint}
-compliant, there exists a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf NicePFamily}({\sf PendantBlueprint},{\sf PendantGuess},{\sf Sep},\sigma)
such that
\sigma^{\prime}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible.

To summarize, in (
DB
,
DT
) case, we can conclude the fate of all the components (i.e., which side of the separator edges they belong to) for
\bigcup_{v\in{\sf Endpoints}({\sf Sep})}{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
. On the other hand, in (
SB
,
ST
) case, we know that, either (i) all except a
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
components in
{\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1))
(resp.
{\sf PeculiarNP}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))
) lie to the left of
{\sf lsep}_{23}
(resp. to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{12}
), or (ii) vice versa. Thus, we can guess which of the two cases we are in, and in each case, two subsets of size
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})
, respectively. This leaves us with (
SB
,
DT
) and (
ST
,
DB
) case, in which case, we know that the
{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
for the shared endpoint
v
are partitioned into
({\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}(v),{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}(v))
; however, we do not know how to find such a partition. The goal of the next subsection is to show that such a partition can be found in a structured way.

5.3.2
Reordering Peculiar Components in (
SB
,
DT
)/(
ST
,
DB
) Case.

Throughout the subsubsection, we will focus on (
SB
,
DT
) case, wherein
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
. The (
ST
,
DB
) case is handled symmetrically by focusing on the corresponidng shared endpoint
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3)
, and by flipping the roles of
V_{1}
and
V_{3}
. We omit the repetition.

We note down the assumptions that will be made throughout this subsubsection. We have a bottom-heavy
k
-minimal instance
\mathcal{I}
with a drawing
\sigma
with
k
crossings, and a
{\sf Sep}
w.r.t.
\sigma
, which is of the case (
SB
,
DT
) case, with
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
, which we denote by
v
throughout the subsection. Further, the vertices
u\in V_{2}
with
\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},2))<\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},2))
and
w\in V_{3}
with
\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},3))\leq\sigma_{3}(w)\leq\sigma_{3}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},3))
will not be relevant for any of the arguments/discussion of this subsubsection, and we will tacitly ignore cases involving such vertices.

Further, let us use
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{l},\mathcal{C}^{r}
instead of
{\sf PeculiarNP},{\sf PeculiarNP}^{l}
,
{\sf PeculiarNP}^{r}
, respectively. Note that these sets are defined w.r.t. a drawing
\sigma
and its corresponding separator
{\sf Sep}
. First, we will analyze the properties of the components of
\mathcal{C}
, then subsequently we will modify
\sigma
to obtain another separator-compatible drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Setup.

Definition 24

(light and heavy components)
.

We say that a component
C\in\mathcal{C}
is
\sigma
-light
if the total number of crossings involving at least one edge of
E(C)\cup\left\{vu:u\in C\right\}
, is at most
\sqrt{k}
; otherwise we say that
C
is
\sigma
-heavy
. We denote the subsets of light and heavy components as
{\sf Heavy}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
and
{\sf Light}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
, respectively.

The following remark is due regarding the simplification of the notation.

Remark 1
.

In the notions introduced in
Definition

24
and later, we initially make the dependence on
v,\mathcal{C}
, and
\sigma
explicit in the notation/terminology, and subsequently simplify it by omitting
v,\mathcal{C},\sigma
from the notation, if the respective objects are obvious from the context (indeed, they are fixed for the rest of the subsection, except for
\sigma
which will be modified at the end). For example, henceforth
{\sf Heavy}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
is abbreviated to simply
{\sf Heavy}
.

We state the following simple observation.

Observation 10
.

The number of heavy components is bounded by
2\sqrt{k}
, i.e.,
|{\sf Heavy}|\leq 2\sqrt{k}
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that
|{\sf Heavy}|>2\sqrt{k}
, then total number of crossings in
\sigma
is more than
2\sqrt{k}\cdot\sqrt{k}/2
, since each
C\in{\sf Heavy}
accounts for more than
\sqrt{k}
crossings, and since each crossing is counted at most twice in this manner (since, for example, the pair of crossing edges may belong to a pair of two different heavy components). This contradicts the fact that
\sigma
is a drawing with
k
crossings.
∎

Leftmost and rightmost light components.

Definition 25

(leftmost and rightmost light components)
.

Let
\prec_{l}
denote a total ordering on
\mathcal{C}
, where for
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
,
C_{1}\prec_{l}C_{2}
if the leftmost neighbor of
v
in
C_{1}
is to the left of the leftmost neighbor of
v
in
C_{2}
. Similarly, let
\prec_{r}
denote a total ordering on
\mathcal{C}
using the rightmost neighbors of
v
in components (note that
C_{1}\prec_{l}C_{2}
does not necessarily imply that
C_{2}\prec_{r}C_{1}
, and vice versa). Let
{\sf LeftmostLC}\in{\sf Light}
be the leftmost light component (according to
\prec_{l}
), and
{\sf RightmostLC}\in{\sf Light}
be the rightmost light component (according to
\prec_{r}
). Note that any component
C
satisfying
C\prec_{l}{\sf LeftmostLC}
or
C\prec_{r}{\sf RightmostLC}
must be a heavy component.

Let
{\sf Extreme}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
denote all the components
C
such that, (i)
C\prec_{l}{\sf LeftmostLC}
, or (ii)
C\prec_{r}{\sf RightmostLC}
, or
(iii) at least one edge of
C
crosses an edge of
{\sf LeftmostLC}
or
{\sf RightmostLC}
.

The following observation follows immediately from the definition.

Observation 11
.

|{\sf Extreme}|\leq 4\sqrt{k}
.

Finally, we define the notion of star vertices, which are crucial to the result shown in this subsubsection.

Definition 26

(Star vertices)
.

we say that a vertex
p\in R_{2}
is a
(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
-left-star vertex
(resp.
(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
-right-star vertex
) if there is an edge incident to
p
that crosses an edge of the form
vu^{l}\in E_{12}
, where
u^{l}\in{\sf LeftmostLC}\cap V_{2}
is the leftmost neighbor of
v
in
{\sf LeftmostLC}
(resp.
u^{r}\in{\sf RightmostLC}\cap V_{2}
is the rightmost neighbor of
v
in
{\sf RightmostLC}
). Let
{\sf LeftStar},{\sf RightStar}
denote the sets of left-star and right-star vertices respectively.
When the left vs. right distinction is not important, we refer to the vertices of
{\sf LeftStar}\cup{\sf RightStar}
as simply
star vertices
. Let
{\sf Star}
denote the resultant set of star vertices, and they are are enumerated as
{\sf st}_{1},{\sf st}_{2},\ldots,{\sf st}_{q}
from left to right according to
\sigma_{2}
. See
Figure

20
for an illustration.

Figure 20:
Illustration of components
{\sf LeftmostLC},{\sf RightmostLC}
and star vertices. Left-star vertices (shown in green) have at least one incident edge crossing
vu_{l}
, and right-star (purple) have at least one incident edge crossing
vu_{r}
. Note that a vertex can be both left- and right-star vertex, e.g.,
p
.

Next, we state and prove following straightforward observations that follow immediately from the definitions.

Observation 12
.

No star vertex belongs to a component in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

A star vertex
u
has an incident edge that crosses an edge incident to
v
, and hence
u
is adjacent to a vertex
w\in V_{1}
, where
w\neq v
. Hence, the connected component in
G-v
that contains
u
, also contains
w\in V_{1}
, implying that
C\in{\sf UpDown}(v)
.
∎

Observation 13
.

|{\sf LeftStar}|,|{\sf RightStar}|\leq\sqrt{k}
.

Proof.

A star vertex has an incident edge that crosses an edge
vu_{l}
for some
u_{l}\in{\sf LeftmostLC}
. However, since
{\sf LeftmostLC}
is a light component, it is involved in at most
\sqrt{k}
crossings. Therefore, the number of vertices in
{\sf LeftStar}
is bounded by
\sqrt{k}
. A similar argument shows that
|S_{r}|\leq\sqrt{k}
.
∎

Figure 21:
Two cases from the proof of
5.1
.

Claim 5.1
.

Let
u,y\in V_{2}
be two distinct vertices such that (1)
u
is a neighbor of
v
, (2) there exists some neighbor
x\in V_{1}
of
y
, and (3) the edges
vu
and
xy
cross each other. Then,
y
must be a star vertex.

Proof.

Since
vu
and
xy
cross, either (i)
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}(x)
and
\sigma_{2}(y)<\sigma_{2}(u)
, or (ii)
\sigma_{1}(v)>\sigma_{1}(x)
and
\sigma_{2}(y)>\sigma_{2}(u)
(See
Figure

21
). In case (i), note that
\sigma_{2}(y)<\sigma_{2}(u)\leq\sigma_{2}(u_{r})
. We also have that
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}(x)
. Therefore,
xy
crosses
vu^{r}
as well, which implies that
y
is a star vertex. Case (ii) is symmetric – we have that
\sigma_{2}(u^{l})\leq\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(y)
, and
\sigma_{2}(x)<\sigma_{2}(v)
. Therefore,
xy
crosses
vu^{l}
implying that
y
is a star vertex.
∎

Figure 22:
Illustration for the proof of
5.2
. In case (i), an edge
xy
that crosses
vu
must also cross
vu^{\prime}
. In case (ii), there are two possible locations for the vertex
y
, shown in orange and purple, respectively.
In the orange case, the edge
xy
again must cross both
vu
and
vu^{\prime}
; whereas in the purple case, the vertex
y
must be a star vertex, contradicting that
{\sf st}^{i}
and
{\sf st}^{i+1}
are consecutive star vertices.

Claim 5.2
.

Let
u,u^{\prime}
be two distinct neighbors of
v
such that
\sigma_{2}({\sf st}_{i})<\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(u^{\prime})<\sigma_{2}({\sf st}_{i+1})
, where
{\sf st}_{i},{\sf st}_{i+1}\in{\sf Star}
are some pair of consecutive star vertices according to
\sigma_{2}
(note that
1\leq i<|{\sf Star}|
). Then, the edges
vu
and
vu^{\prime}
cross the same set of edges in
\sigma
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists an edge
xy
with
x\in V_{1},y\in V_{2}
, such that
xy
crosses
vu
but not
vu^{\prime}
(the other case is symmetric). Since
xy
crosses
vu
, it must be the case that (i)
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}(x)
and
\sigma_{2}(u)>\sigma_{2}(y)
, or (ii)
\sigma_{1}(v)>\sigma_{1}(x)
and
\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(y)
. See
Figure

22
.

First, consider case (i). Since
\sigma_{2}(y)<\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(u^{\prime})
and
\sigma_{1}(v)<\sigma_{1}(x)
, it follows that
vu^{\prime}
and
xy
cross as well.

Now consider case (ii). We have that
\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(u^{\prime})
,
\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(y)
, and
\sigma_{1}(x)<\sigma_{1}(v)
. If
\sigma_{2}(u^{\prime})<\sigma_{2}(y)
, then
xy
crosses
vu^{\prime}
. Otherwise,
\sigma_{2}(x_{l})\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf st}_{i})<\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}(u^{\prime})<\sigma_{2}(y)\leq\sigma_{2}({\sf st}^{i+1})\leq\sigma_{2}(x_{r})
. However, by
5.1
, we obtain that
y
must be a star vertex, which contradicts that
{\sf st}_{i+1}
is the next consecutive star vertex after
{\sf st}_{i}
.
∎

Next, we introduce the notion of components that “surround” a star vertex, and then bound the number of such components.

Definition 27
.

We say that a component
C

surrounds
a star vertex
{\sf st}
, if it contains two vertices that are placed to the either side of
{\sf st}
in
\sigma_{2}
(see
Figure

23
). Let
{\sf Surround}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\setminus({\sf Heavy}\cup{\sf Extreme})
denote the subset of components
C
such that
C
surrounds some
u\in{\sf Star}
. Note that no pendant component can belong to
{\sf Surround}
.

Figure 23:
Illustration for the proof of
5.3
. Components
C_{1}
(blue) and
C_{2}
(red) surround a star vertex
{\sf st}
(
C_{1}
happens to surround
{\sf st}^{\prime}
as well). Due to the positional assumptions in the first case in the proof of
5.3
, edges
y_{1}w_{1}
and
x_{2}w_{2}
must cross (shown as thick dashed edges).

We show the following claim regarding the necessity of crossings between components surrounding the same star vertex.

Claim 5.3
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\in{\sf Surround}
be distinct components that surround a star vertex
{\sf st}\in{\sf Star}
. Then, there exist edges
e_{1}\in E(C_{1})
and
e_{2}\in E(C_{2})
that cross each other.

Proof.

Since
C_{1}
surrounds
{\sf st}
, it contains two vertices that are placed to the either side of
{\sf st}
. Further, since
C_{1}
is a connected component in
G-v
, it must contain a vertex
w_{1}\in V_{3}\cap V(C_{1})
such that
w_{1}
has two neighbors in
C_{1}
, say
x_{1}
and
y_{1}
such that
x_{1}
is to the left of
{\sf st}
, and
y_{1}
is to the right of
{\sf st}
. Similarly, define
w_{2},x_{2},y_{2}\in V(C_{2})
, respectively.

Suppose first that
\sigma_{3}(w_{1})<\sigma_{3}(w_{2})
, i.e.,
w_{1}
is to the left of
w_{2}
. Then, since
\sigma_{2}(x_{2})<\sigma_{2}({\sf st})<\sigma_{2}(y_{1})
, the edges
x_{2}w_{2}
and
y_{1}w_{1}
cross each other (see
Figure

23
). In the other case, when
\sigma_{3}(w_{2})<\sigma_{3}(w_{1})
, we can observe symmetrically that the edges
x_{1}w_{1}
and
y_{2}w_{2}
cross each other. This proves the claim.
∎

In the following lemma, we bound the number of components that surround some star vertex.

Lemma 16
.

|{\sf Surround}|\leq 5\cdot(k)^{2/3}
.

Proof.

Let us number the left-star vertices from right to left (in
\sigma_{2}
) as
{\sf st}^{l}_{1},{\sf st}^{l}_{2},\ldots
, and analogously, let us number the right-star vertices from left to right as
{\sf st}^{r}_{1},{\sf st}^{r}_{2},\ldots
. Let
t=(k)^{1/3}
, and let
{\sf Star}^{\prime}=\left\{{\sf st}^{l}_{i}\in{\sf LeftStar}:i\leq t\right\}\cup\left\{{\sf st}^{r}_{i}\in{\sf RightStar}:i\leq t\right\}
. Note that
|{\sf Star}^{\prime}|\leq 2t=2(k)^{2/3}
. In order to bound the size of
{\sf Surround}
, we classify each
C\in{\sf Surround}
into two types:
C
is class 1 if it surrounds some star vertex of
{\sf Star}^{\prime}
; otherwise, if it only surrounds some vertex of
{\sf Star}\setminus{\sf Star}^{\prime}
, then it is class 2. In the following two claims, we bound the number of components of each class separately.

Claim 5.4
.

The number of class 1 components in
{\sf Surround}
is at most
4\cdot(k)^{2/3}
.

Proof of
5.4
.

We arbitrarily number the vertices of
{\sf Star}^{\prime}
as
{\sf st}^{\prime}_{1},{\sf st}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,{\sf st}^{\prime}_{z}
, where
z=|{\sf Star}^{\prime}|\leq 2(k^{1/3})
. Let
n_{i}
denote the number of components that surround
{\sf st}^{\prime}_{i}
, and w.l.o.g. assume
n_{i}>0
for each
i
– otherwise such star vertex plays no role in the following analysis, and can be ignored. Let
m_{i}=n_{i}-1\geq 0
for each
1\leq i\leq z
. Hence, the total number of class 1 components is at most
\sum_{i=1}^{z}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}+z
. Since
z\leq 2(k)^{2/3}
, it suffices to show that
\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}\leq 2\cdot(k)^{2/3}
.

By
5.3
any pair of components that surround the same
{\sf st}_{i}\in{\sf Star}
must have a pair of crossing edges. Therefore, the number of crossings among the components surrounding
{\sf st}_{i}
is at least
\frac{n_{i}(n_{i}-1)}{2}\geq\frac{m_{i}^{2}}{2}
. However, since there are at most
k
crossings, it follows that
\sum_{i=1}^{z}\frac{m_{i}^{2}}{2}\leq k
, which implies that,
\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}^{2}\leq 2k
. Now, by using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}^{2}\right)\cdot z\leq 2kz.

This implies that,
\sum_{i=1}^{z}m_{i}\leq\sqrt{2kz}\leq\sqrt{2k\cdot 2(k)^{1/3}}=2\cdot\sqrt{(k)^{4/3}}=2\cdot(k)^{2/3}
.
∎

Claim 5.5
.

The number of class 2 components in
{\sf Surround}
is at most
(k)^{2/3}
.

Proof of
5.5
.

We will show that each component of class 2 is involved in at least
(k)^{1/3}
distinct relevant crossings, which immediately implies the lemma.

Fix one such class 2 component
C
, and note that it surrounds a star vertex
{\sf st}\not\in{\sf Star}^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, suppose
{\sf st}
is a left-star vertex (the right-star case is symmetric), say
{\sf st}^{l}_{i}\in{\sf LeftStar}\setminus{\sf Star}^{\prime}
, which implies that
i>t
. Further, since
C
is not a class 1 component, it does not surround any
{\sf st}^{l}_{i^{\prime}}
with
i^{\prime}\leq t
. However,
C
contains a neighbor
w_{l}
of
v
, and by assumption, it also lies to the left of all of
{\sf st}^{l}_{i^{\prime}}
with
i^{\prime}\leq t
.

Since
C
is a light component, it holds that
{\sf LeftmostLC}\prec_{l}C
, where recall that
{\sf LeftmostLC}
is the leftmost component from
Definition

25
, with
u^{l}\in{\sf LeftStar}
being the leftmost neighbor of
v
. Similarly, let
w^{l}
denote teh leftmost neighbor of
v
in
C
. This implies that
\sigma(u^{l})<\sigma(w^{l})<\sigma({\sf st}^{l}_{i^{\prime}})
for all
i^{\prime}\leq t
. Further, since an edge
e
incident to
{\sf st}^{l}_{i^{\prime}}
crosses
vu_{l}
, the other endpoint of
e
, say
u_{b}\in V_{1}
lies to the left of
v
. However, this implies that
e
also crosses the edge
vw^{l}
(See
Figure

24
). Since this holds for each
i\leq t
, the edge
vw^{l}
crosses at least
t
distinct edges, thus accounting for at least
t
distinct crossings.

∎

With
5.4
and
5.5
, we conclude the proof of
Lemma

16
.

∎

Figure 24:
Illustration for
5.5
. Component
C
(shown in red) surrounds star vertex
{\sf st}^{l}_{i}\not\in{\sf Star}^{\prime}
. For each
i^{\prime}\leq k^{1/3}
, the edge
vw_{l}
(dashed red) crosses all edges incident to
{\sf st}^{l}_{i^{\prime}}
that also cross the edge
vu^{l}
(blue). Thus,
C
accounts for at least
(k)^{1/3}
crossings.

Component Types.

Next, we define the notion of a type of a component.

Definition 28

(Original and reduced type)
.

Let
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}(C)=\mathcal{C}\setminus{\sf Pendants}(v)
be a component in
G-v
. Let
{\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C)\coloneqq|M^{\sf orig}_{1}2||\left\{uv:u\in C\cap V_{2}\right\}|
, i.e., the number of edges between
v
and the vertices of
C
(including multiplicities), and
{\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C)\coloneqq|E(C)|
, i.e., the number of edges in
C
.

Then, the
original type
of
C
is defined as the pair
({\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C),{\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C))
, and the
reduced type
of
C
is defined as
({\sf m}_{12}(C),{\sf m}_{23}(C))\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,(k)^{1/3},\ast,L\right\}^{2}
, where

({\sf m}_{12}(C),{\sf m}_{23}(C))=\begin{cases}({\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C),{\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C))&\text{ if $0<{\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C)<(k)^{1/3}$ and ${\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C)<(k)^{1/3}$}\\
(\ast,L)&\text{ if ${\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C)\geq(k)^{1/3}$}\\
(L,\ast)&\text{ if ${\sf m_{23}^{orig}}(C)<(k)^{1/3}$ and ${\sf m_{12}^{orig}}\geq(k)^{1/3}$.}\end{cases}

Here, the meaning of
(\ast,L)
type is that
|E_{23}|
is
larger
than
(k)^{1/3}
(hence,
L
), whereas the number of neighbors of
v
in
C
is arbitrary (hence,
\ast
). We interpret
(L,\ast)
similarly.

For any pendant
C=\left\{u\right\}\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
. Then, with
{\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C)
denote the multiplicity of the edge
vu
. Then, the original type of
C
is defined to be
({\sf m_{12}^{orig}}(C),0)
, and the reduced type of
C
is defined as
(1,0)
.

Finally, we define
\mathsf{Types}\coloneqq\left\{1,\ldots,(k)^{1/3}\right\}\times\left\{1,2,\ldots,(k)^{1/3}\right\}\cup\left\{(\ast,L),(L,\ast),(1,0)\right\}
.

The following observations are immediate from the definition of original/reduced types.

Observation 14
.

1.

|\mathsf{Types}|\leq k^{2/3}+3=\mathcal{O}(k^{2/3})
.

2.

C\in{\sf Pendants}(v)
, iff the original type of
C
is
(i,0)
for some
i\geq 1
. Otherwise,
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
iff the original type of
C
is
(i,j)
with
i,j>0
.

3.

The original/reduced type of a component entirely depends on its structural properties, and is
independent
of a drawing.

Definition 29

(Exceptional and nice components)
.

Let
{\sf Exceptional}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)\coloneqq{\sf Heavy}\cup{\sf Extreme}\cup{\sf Surround}
, and let
{\sf nice}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)\coloneqq\mathcal{C}\setminus{\sf Exceptional}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
.
In the drawing
\sigma
, for each
(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}
, the set of all
nice components
of reduced type
(i,j)
is denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}(i,j)
, and for each
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, the set of all nice components of original type
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is denoted by
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\sigma}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
20
20

20

Note that the sets
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot),\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
are subsets of
{\sf nice}(v,\mathcal{C},\sigma)
and hence depend on the drawing
\sigma
.

From
10
,
11
, and
Lemma

16
, we immediately obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

|{\sf Exceptional}|\leq 11\cdot(k)^{2/3}=\mathcal{O}(k^{2/3})
.

Rearrangement of Nice Components

Now we are left with analyzing the behavior of nice components. Note that by definition, each
C\in{\sf nice}(\mathcal{C})
is a light component, and it does not surround any star vertex. We need the following definition to analyze the behavior of such nice components.

Definition 30

(Slot)
.

Let us now order the star vertices of
{\sf Star}
from left to right as
{\sf st}_{1},{\sf st}_{2},\ldots,{\sf st}_{q}
, and we define set of vertices
{\sf slot}_{i}(\sigma)\coloneqq\left\{u\in V_{2}:\sigma_{2}({\sf st}_{i})<\sigma_{2}(u)<\sigma_{2}({\sf st}_{i+1})\right\}
as an
{\sf Star}
-slot
, or simply a
slot
, defined by two consecutive star vertices
{\sf st}_{i},{\sf st}_{i+1}
. We say that a component
C

belongs to
a slot
{\sf slot}_{i}(\sigma)
in
\sigma
, if
V(C)\cap V_{2}\subseteq{\sf slot}_{i}(\sigma)
.

The following observation follows from the definition of
{\sf nice}(\mathcal{C})
.

Observation 15
.

Each
C\in{\sf nice}
belongs to some slot
{\sf slot}_{i}(\sigma)
.

We further define the following notion – note that this is defined w.r.t.
any
drawing
\varsigma=(\varsigma_{1},\varsigma_{2},\varsigma_{3})
, not
just
for a drawing
\sigma
fixed at the beginning of the section.

Definition 31

(Consecutiveness)
.

•

We say that a set of vertices
Q
is
consecutive
in
\varsigma
if for each
i\in[3]
,
Q_{i}\coloneqq Q\cap V_{i}
satisfies the following property: (a) either
|Q_{i}|\leq 1
, or (b) there exist two distinct vertices
u_{l},u_{r}\in Q_{i}
, such that
Q_{i}=\left\{u\in V_{i}:\varsigma_{i}(u_{l})\leq\varsigma_{i}(u)\leq\varsigma_{i}(u_{r})\right\}
.

•

We say that a collection of vertex-subsets
{\cal Q}=\left\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\ldots,Q_{\ell}\right\}
is
consecutive
in
\varsigma
, if (a)
\bigcup_{Q_{j}\in{\cal Q}}Q_{j}
is consecutive in
\varsigma
, and moreover (b) each
Q_{j}\in{\cal Q}
is consecutive in
\varsigma
.
21
21

21

Note that these two conditions together imply that the sets
Q_{j}\in{\cal Q}
are placed “one after the other” in some arbitrary order, and no vertex not in any of
Q_{j}
’s is placed anywhere “in between” the sets.

In the following definitions, we introduce the notion of a guess.

Definition 32
.

For each
C\in{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
, let
{\sf opt}(C)
denote the optimal number of crossings required to draw
C
on
2
lines. Further, for a subset
{\cal S}\subseteq{\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
, let
{\sf opt}({\cal S})\coloneqq\sum_{C\in{\cal S}}{\sf opt}(C)
.

Definition 33
.

A
component-blueprint
is a tuple
{\sf ComponentBlueprint}=({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum},{\sf optL},{\sf optR},\mathcal{E})
, where

•

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(L,\ast)\cup\mathcal{T}(\ast,L)\subseteq{\sf nice}(\mathcal{C})
is a subset of components of size at most
k^{2/3}
.

•

{\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum}:\mathsf{Types}\to\mathbb{N}
, where, for each
(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}
, it holds that,
{\sf LeftNum}(i,j)+{\sf RightNum}(i,j)=|\mathcal{T}(i,j)\setminus\mathcal{E}|
.

•

{\sf optL},{\sf optR}:\mathsf{Types}\to\mathbb{N}
, where, for each
(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}
, it holds that,
{\sf optL}(i,j)+{\sf optR}(i,j)={\sf opt}(\mathcal{T}(i,j)\setminus\mathcal{E})
.

Finally, we define the notion of blueprint-compliant guess.

Definition 34
.

A
component-guess
is a tuple
{\sf ComponentGuess}=({\sf LeftComps},{\sf RightComps})
where,
{\sf LeftComps},{\sf RightComps}:\mathsf{Types}\to\mathcal{C}
where for each
(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}
,
{\sf LeftComps}(i,j),{\sf RightComps}(i,j)
are disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{T}(i,j)
.

Let
{\sf ComponentGuess}=({\sf LeftComps},{\sf RightComps})
be a component-guess, and
{\sf ComponentBlueprint}=({\sf LeftNum},{\sf RightNum},{\sf optL},{\sf optR},\mathcal{E})
be a component-blueprint. We say that the guess
{\sf ComponentGuess}
is
{\sf ComponentBlueprint}
-compliant
if, for each
(i,j)\in\mathcal{T})
,

•

|{\sf LeftComps}(i,j)|={\sf LeftNum}(i,j)
, and
|{\sf RightComps}(i,j)|={\sf RightNum}(i,j)
,

•

{\sf opt}({\sf LeftComps}(i,j))={\sf optL}(i,j)
, and
{\sf opt}({\sf RightComps}(i,j))={\sf optR}(i,j)
.

Definition 35
.

Let
{\cal G}=({\sf LeftComps},{\sf RightComps})
be a guess. We define a family

{\sf NiceCFamily}({\sf ComponentBlueprint},{\sf ComponentGuess},{\sf Sep},\sigma)
to be a family of drawings
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying the following properties:

•

\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
, when respected to
V(G)\setminus\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}}({\sf LeftComps}(i,j)\cup{\sf RightComps}(i,j))
are equal, and

•

Let
V_{L}\coloneqq\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}}{\sf LeftComps}(i,j)
and
V_{R}\coloneqq\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{Types}}{\sf RightComps}(i,j)
. Then, for each
\ell\in\left\{2,3\right\},\sigma_{\ell}(u)<\sigma_{\ell}({\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},\ell))
for each
u\in V_{L}\cap V_{\ell}
, and
\sigma_{\ell}(w)>\sigma_{\ell}({\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},\ell))
for each
w\in V_{R}\cap V_{\ell}
.

Finally, we state the main lemma of our section.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
k
-minimal bottom-heavy
Yes
-instance, let
\sigma
be a drawing with
k
crossings. Let
{\sf Sep}(\sigma)
be a separator w.r.t.
\sigma
in (
SB
,
DT
) case, where
v\coloneqq{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{12},1)={\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{12},1)
. Then, there exists a blueprint
{\sf ComponentBlueprint}
, such that, for any
{\sf ComponentBlueprint}
-compliant guess
{\sf ComponentGuess}
, there exists a drawing
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
(i)
\sigma\in{\sf NiceCFamily}(\allowbreak{\sf ComponentBlueprint},{\sf ComponentGuess},{\sf Sep},\sigma)
, and (ii)
\sigma^{\prime}
is
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible.

Proof.

First, let us introduce some notation that will be used throughout the lemma.
Let
{\sf nice}\coloneqq{\sf nice}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathcal{C}={\sf PeculiarNP}(v)
. Let
{\sf CompEdges}=\bigcup_{C\in{\sf nice}}E(C)
, and
{\sf ConnEdges}=\bigcup_{C\in{\sf nice}}{\sf ConnEdges}(C)
, where
{\sf ConnEdges}(C)\coloneqq\left\{vu:u\in C\cap V_{2}\right\}
.

For a drawing
\varsigma
, we say that a component
C\in{\sf nice}
is
\varsigma
-left (resp.
\varsigma
-right), if in the drawing
\varsigma
, for
\ell\in\left\{2,3\right\}
, all the vertices of
C\cap V_{\ell}
are to the left of
{\sf endpt}({\sf lsep}_{23},\ell)
(resp. to the right of
{\sf endpt}({\sf rsep}_{23},\ell)
).

Transforming
\sigma
.

In the beginning,
\sigma^{\prime}
is initialized to
\sigma
. Throughout this proof, we will gradually transform
\sigma
by reordering the vertices involved in
R\coloneqq\bigcup_{C\in{\sf nice}}C
, while keeping the relative ordering of the rest of the vertices unchanged. This will be done in multiple steps. Before this, however, we set up things.
Further, during this process, we will also ensure that,
\sigma^{\prime}
remains
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-compatible (note that in the beginning,
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
and thus this property holds initially). Specifically, during each step of this process, we ensure the following:

1.

Only
\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\sigma^{\prime}_{3}
are modified by only changing the relative ordering between the vertices of
R\cap V_{2}
and
R\cap V_{3}
, respectively, whereas
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}
remains unchanged.

2.

The sets of left- and right-star vertices w.r.t.
v,\mathcal{C},\sigma^{\prime}
remain unchanged throughout the procedure.

3.

The number of crossings remains the same, and moreover, the number of crossings on the left of
{\sf lsep}_{12},{\sf lsep}_{23}
, and that to the right of
{\sf rsep}_{12},{\sf rsep}_{23}
, remains the same.

4.

for any nice component
C\in{\sf nice}(\mathcal{C})
, the number of crossings involving at least one edge of
E(C)
does not increase.

At the end, when we will have achieved property 2, it follows that the final drawing

\sigma^{\prime}
at that point will be a
({\sf Sep},\sigma)
-well-behaved drawing.

This gradual transformation can be broadly divided into three steps, which we explain in detail below.

Step 1. Making each
C\in{\sf nice}
consecutive.

By
15
, each
C\in{\sf nice}
belongs to a slot
S_{i}
. Now, let
e=uw\in E(C)\subseteq E_{23}
(with
u\in V_{2},w\in V_{3}
) be an edge crosses the minimum number of edges outside
E(C)
. Let
E_{out}\subseteq E_{23}\setminus E(C)
denote the set of edges that cross
e
. Let
p^{l}(C)\in V_{2}\setminus C
(resp.
p^{r}(C)\in V_{2}\setminus C
) be the rightmost (resp. leftmost) vertex in
V_{2}\setminus C
according to
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
that is to the left (resp. right) of
u
. Similarly, define
q^{l}(C),q^{r}(C)\in V_{3}\setminus C
w.r.t.
w
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{3}
. Then, we modify
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
by moving all the vertices of
C\cap V_{2}
between
p^{l}(C)
and
p^{r}(C)
, while maintaining the same relative order among themselves. Similarly, we move all the vertices of
C\cap V_{3}
between
q^{l}(C)
and
q^{r}(C)
while maintaining the same relative order among themselves. This ensures that
C
is consecutive in the resulting drawing
\varsigma
.

We now argue that the number of crossings does not increase after the transformation. First, note that by
15
,
V(C)\cap V_{2}\subseteq{\sf slot}_{i}
for some slot
{\sf slot}_{i}
, and we only move the vertices of
C\cap V_{2}
within the same slot. Therefore, via an argument similar to
5.2
, for each edge
e_{12}\in{\sf ConnEdges}(C)
, the set of edges crossing
e_{12}
does not change due to the modification. Further, since we maintain the same relative order among the vertices of
V(C)\cap V_{2}
and
V(C)\cap V_{3}
, the set of edges of
E(C)
that cross any edge
e_{23}
also does not change. On the other hand, for each
